PREFACE 
This Report for the period ended 31 March 2023 has been prepared for submission to 
Lieutenant Governor of Jammu and Kashmir under  Section 72 of  the Jammu and 
Kashmir Reorganisation Act 2019 for laying in the Legislative Assembly.  
This Report comprises three parts and includes seven chapters.  
Part-A of this Report  includes five chapters dealing  with the summary of financial 
performance of Public Sector Enterprises, oversight role of Comptroller and Auditor 
General, corporate governance, corporate social responsibility in respect of Government 
companies and statutory corporations for the period ended 31 March 2023. 
Part-B of this Report includes one chapter contai ning the significant audit findings 
noticed during the Performance Audit on Execution of Works under NABARD-RIDF 
by Public Works and Jal Shakti Departments.  
Part-C of this Report includes one chapter containing the significant audit findings 
noticed during the Information Technology (IT) Audit of ‘Integrated Financial 
Management System (IFMS)’ in Jammu and Kashmir. 
The instances mentioned in this Report are those which came to the notice in the 
course of test audit of the period 2022 -23 as well as those which came to notice in 
earlier years but could not be reported in the previous Audit Reports. Instances 
relating to the period subsequent to 2022 -23 have also been included, wherever 
necessary.  
The audit has been conducted in conformity with the Auditing Standards issued by the 
Comptroller and Auditor General of India. 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
EXECUTIVE SUMMARY 
  

ix 
EXECUTIVE SUMMARY 
About the Report  
This Report comprises three parts and includes seven chapters.  
Part A of this Report deals with the summary of financial performance of Public Sector 
Enterprises, oversight role of Comptroller and Auditor General, corporate governance, 
corporate social responsibility in respect  of Government companies and statutory 
corporations for the period ended 31 March 2023. 
Part-B of this Report includes significant audit findings noticed during the Performance 
Audit on Execution of Works under NABARD-RIDF by Public Works and Jal Shakti 
Departments.  
Part-C of this Report includes one chapter containing the significant audit findings 
noticed during the Information Technology (IT) Audit of Integrated Financial 
Management System (IFMS) in Jammu and Kashmir Government. 
The primary purpose of this Report is to bring to the notice of the Legislature the 
important results of audit.  
The Report has been organised in seven Chapters as under:  
Chapter-I: Functioning of UT Public Sector Enterprises  
This Chapter contains brief details of Union Territory Public Sector Enterprises 
(UTPSEs). As on 31 March 2023, there were 42 Public Sector Enterprises ( PSEs) in 
Jammu and Kashmir under the audit jurisdiction of the CAG. These included  
39 Government Companies, two Statutory Corporations 1 and one Government 
Controlled Other Company2. 
Chapter-II: Financial Performance of UT Public Sector Enterprises 
Out of 42 UTPSEs in UT of Jammu and Kashmir including two Statutory corporations; 
six UTPSEs were inactive and 36 were active UTPSEs. The thrust of PSE investment 
was on power sector which attracted 78 per cent of the total investment. Eleven UT 
PSEs were profit making as per their latest finalized accounts and  only one UTPSE 
(Jammu & Kashmir Bank Limited)  had declared/ paid dividend. The reported profit 
increased to ` 1,286.79 crore in 2022 -23 from ` 566.10 crore in 2021 -22. Twenty 
UTPSEs had reported losses (` 252.53 crore in 2022 -23) in their latest finalised 
accounts and net worth of 14 out of 19 PSEs had been completely eroded by their 
accumulated loss. 
 
1 Jammu and Kashmir Road Transport Corporation and Jammu and Kasmir and Ladakh Financial 
Corporation 
2 Chenab Valley Power Projects (Private) Limited ( CVPPPL), a joint venture of Jammu and Kashmir 
Power Development Corporation (JKPDC), National Hydroelectric Power Corporation (NHPC) and 
Power Trading Corporation (PTC). 

Composite Audit Report (Civil and PSEs) for the period ended March 2023 
x 
Recommendations 
• The UT Government may take an early decision regarding commencement of 
liquidation process in respect of the inactive PSEs as they are neither 
contributing to economy nor meeting the objectives for which they were 
set up. 
• The UT Government may take remedial actions to improve the performance 
of loss making UTPSEs.  
Chapter-III: Oversight Role of CAG 
Out of 34 UTPSEs in UT of Jammu and Kashmir for which accounts for 2022-23 were 
due for audit; only four UTPSEs presented their Financial statements for CAG’s audit 
by 30 September 2023. Nineteen UTPSEs are those with accounts in arrears for 
three years and more. The accounts of  two Statutory Corporations Jammu & Kashmir 
Road Transport Corporation and Jammu & Kashmir and Ladakh Financial Corporation 
were in arrears for two years and more as on 30 September 2023. 
Recommendation 
• The Government may direct PSEs with arrear in accounts to ensure early 
finalisation of their Financial Statements as in the absence of their 
finalisation, Government investments in such PSEs remain outside the 
legislative oversight. 
Chapter-IV: Corporate Governance 
Audit reviewed 33 UTPSEs and observed that although ten UTPSEs were required to 
appoint Independent Directors, only one UTPSE (J&K Bank Limited) had appointed  
the required number of Independent Directors during 2022 -23. Out of 33 UTPSEs, 
ten UTPSEs were required to constitute an Audit Committee and Nomination and 
Remuneration Committee, however, only one UTPSE, J&K Bank limited had formed 
such committees. Only two out of eight eligible UTPSEs had appointed a whole time 
Company Secretary. In one UTPSE, the work of Company Secretary was being carried 
out by part -time Company Secretary and in remaining five UTPSEs, no Company 
Secretary had been appointed. Only nine out of 33 UTPSEs had conducted the required 
number of BoD meetings during 2022 -23. AGM was not conducted in 24 out of 
33 UTPSEs, during 2022-23. 
Recommendation 
• The Government of Jammu and Kashmir may direct the respective 
Administrative Departments to ensure compliance with the Companies Act 
2013 to achieve the objectives of Corporate Governance in UTPSEs.  The 
Board of Directors of the respective UTPSEs may also ensure compliance 
with the relevant provisions of the Companies Act 2013. 

Executive Summary 
xi 
Chapter-V: Corporate Social Responsibility 
Out of nine UTPSEs in UT of Jammu and Kashmir, which met the criteria required to 
undertake CSR activities as per the Act, only four UTPSEs have constituted CSR 
Committees and have their CSR policy. Seven UTPSEs were required to incur 
expenditure during 2022-23, of which only three UTPSEs had incurred expenditure on 
CSR activities. 
Recommendations 
• All UTPSEs meeting the criteria may frame CSR Policy and constitute 
CSR Committee in compliance of provisions of the Companies Act, 2013 and 
CSR Rules.  
• UTPSEs required to undertake CSR activities may ensure requisite 
allocations are made and utilised as per provisions of the Companies Act, 
2013. 
Chapter-VI: Performance Audit on Execution of Works under NABARD -RIDF 
by Public Works and Jal Shakti Departments 
Performance Audit on ‘Execution of Works under NABARD -RIDF by Public Works 
and Jal Shakti Departments’, covering the period from April 2018 to March 2023, was 
conducted to ascertain whether planning process for selection of projects for funding 
under NABARD was adequate and effective; funds were effectively utilised and 
projects were executed efficiently and effectively with due regard to economy; and 
internal controls and monitoring mechanisms were adequate and effective. 
Against GoI consent for raising loans of ` 3,900 crore from NABARD, GoJ&K availed 
only ` 2,316.37 crore (59 per cent ) during 2018 -19 to 2022 -23. Disbursement by 
NABARD ranged between 28 and 84 per cent of the loans sanctioned across tranches, 
primarily due to slow progress of works. In several cases, loans lapsed due to  
non-adherence to prescribed timelines. 
There were instances of lapses by the Finance Department in managing the 
Mobilisation Advance (MA), including delays in its release to implementing 
departments, as well as cases of non -release or excess disbursement.  Cases of  
non-utilisation of MA by implementing departments  were also noticed. These 
deficiencies adversely affected cash flow and timely execution of projects. 
Audit observed that project planning and prioritisation were weak across the executing 
departments. Baseline data and State/ UT-level planning frameworks envisaged under 
NABARD guidelines were either absent or not effectively used for project selection. 
Mandatory pre -requisites such as availability of encumbrance -free land, forest and 
statutory clearances, identification of viable water sources, and approval of technically 
sound DPRs were not ensured before sanction or execution, leading to stalled, 
abandoned or foreclosed projects. 

Composite Audit Report (Civil and PSEs) for the period ended March 2023 
xii 
Execution of projects suffered from systemic deficiencies, including delays in release 
of funds, non-commencement and slow progress of works, diversion and misutilisation 
of funds, execution without Technical Sanction, defective DPRs, improper site 
selection and inadequate contract management. Monitoring and inspection mechanisms 
were weak, as periodic field inspections were either not conducted or not documented. 
As a result,  32 projects (12 per cent) out of 263 projects due for completion  under 
NABARD as on March 2023  could be completed, while 231 projects were either not 
started or were incomplete as of March 2023. Further, against a total release of  
` 844.17 crore  during 2018 -23, expenditure of ` 382.50 crore  (45 per cent ) was 
incurred, leaving ` 461.67 crore  unutilised. The unutilised portion of released funds 
during each year ranged between four and 72 per cent.  
Consequently, the intended objectives of irrigation and flood management, provision 
of safe drinking water and improvement of rural road connectivity  were not achieved 
within the envisaged timeframes. Overall, there is need for strengthening institutional 
planning, financial discipline, inter -departmental coordination, technical scrutiny and 
monitoring mechanisms to ensure effective utilisation of NABA RD assistance and 
timely delivery of intended benefits. 
The citizen -centric objective of NABARD funding to provide low -cost funding to 
State/ UT Governments for completing delayed rural infrastructure projects, bridging 
the rural urban gap and promoting inclusive growth, covering areas like irrigation, 
roads, health, education and water, is yet to be realized. 
Recommendations 
The Government may: 
• Prescribe timelines for release of Mobilisation Advance to Implementing 
Departments by Finance Department in sanction order, in consultation with 
NABARD.  
• Develop and implement system to prevent occurrence of release of 
Mobilisation Advance twice for same project. 
• Ensure pre-execution approvals from various authorities before initiating the 
projects. 
Chapter-VII: Information Technology (IT) Audit of Integrated Financial 
Management System (IFMS) in Jammu and Kashmir  
The Integrated Financial Management System (IFMS) was envisaged under Mission 
Mode Project (MMP) of the National e-Governance Plan (NeGP). It is a comprehensive 
financial management system for ensuring effective  financial management and  
real-time monitoring of the State’s fiscal position. Government of Jammu and Kashmir 
(GoJ&K) took up (March 2010) the implementation of IFMS. 

Executive Summary 
xiii 
The Business Process Re-engineering process was deficient and GoJ&K had not formed 
Core Consultant Group as of March 2023 for implementation of IFMS in consideration 
of user requirements. Due to non -revision of DPR as per GoI’s instructions and  
non-achievement of prescribed milestones of MMP -Treasury computerisation 
guidelines, the State/  UT had lost the central assistance of ` 11.88 crore for 
implementation of IFMS. Instead of carrying out upgradation of S tate Data Centre 
(SDC) for hosting/storage of IFMS applications, NIC irregularly hosted all the 
applications of IFMS on NIC Mini Data Centre at Jammu without any supporting 
backup site while the funding (` 5.26 crore) for the same remained misutilised. 
The implementation of IFMS in J&K is characterised by various deficiencies and 
significant delays. As of March 2023, only four out of the twelve proposed modules 
were fully operational, leading to delay of more than a decade in full implementation 
of IFMS.  In BEAMS and JKPaySys modules, there were shortcomings in data 
validation, which further compromise system integrity and usability. The lack of  
client-side and server -side validation may lead  to submission of erroneous and 
incomplete data impacting overall data management. Lack of integration among key 
financial applications namely BEAMS and TreasuryNet reflect the technical 
inefficiency of the system in ensuring seamless data transfer. This n ot only resulted in 
excess expenditure over allocation but also rendered the system susceptible to 
unauthorised modifications in bill particulars.  
There was absence of robust logical access controls, effective password recovery 
options and transaction log in which creates opportunities for unauthorised access and 
manipulation of sensitive information. System does not provide for complete trail of 
the transactions such as who inserted the records and who modified them.  Lack of a 
comprehensive Disaster Recovery and Business Continuity Plan exposes the system to 
risks of data integrity and availability in case of unforeseen events like cyber -attacks 
and operational disruptions. Administrative control of IFMS was not taken over by the 
DGAT even after the lapse of over 12 years. 
The absence of User Acceptance Testing (except in case of TreasuryNet in 2011) raises 
concerns about whether the software adequately meets user requirements and functions 
effectively. The inability of the department to secure certifications from the 
Standardisation Testing and Quality Certificationagency  indicates that the project did 
not adhere to industry standards for quality assurance, risk assessment, and 
management certification. 
Recommendations 
GoJ&K may consider: 
• Establishing Core Consultant Group of experts and consultants for proper 
guidance, monitoring, and decision -making during the project’s 
implementation. 

Composite Audit Report (Civil and PSEs) for the period ended March 2023 
xiv 
• Conducting a comprehensive Business Process Re -engineering and 
Requirements Analysis to align the IFMS system with the modern business 
rules and practices. 
• Ensuring strict compliance with established guidelines for user authentication, 
data submission and financial transactions.  
• Ensure timely integration among IFMS applications for data integrity. 
• Securing access controls by implementation of role -based access control and 
Multi-Factor Authentication. 
• Maintaining Audit trail of all transactions or data modifications be maintained. 
• Developing a Disaster Recovery Plan by introducing backup strategies, 
recovery time objectives and recovery point objectives. 
• Ensuring certification from the Standardisation Testing and Quality 
Certification (STQC) agency to demonstrate adherence to industry -standard 
practices. 

PART-A 
PUBLIC SECTOR ENTERPRISES 

CHAPTER-I 
FUNCTIONING OF UT PUBLIC 
SECTOR ENTERPRISES 

1 
 
CHAPTER-I 
FUNCTIONING OF UT PUBLIC SECTOR ENTERPRISES 
1.1  General 
Union Territory Public Sector Enterprises (UTPSEs) consist of UT Government 
Companies and Statutory Corporations. UTPSEs are established to carry out activities 
of commercial nature keeping in view the  welfare of people and occupy an important 
place in the UT economy. This Report discusses the financial performance of UT 
Government of Jammu and Kashmir (GoJ&K) owned Companies, Statutory 
Corporations and Government Controlled Other Companies (GCOCs). Impact of 
significant comments issued after conducting supplementary audit of the financial 
statements of these Public Sector Enterprises (PSEs) by the Comptroller and Audi tor 
General (CAG) of India for the year 2022 -23 (or of earlier years which were finalised 
during the year 2023-24) has also been discussed in the Report. As on 31 March 2023, 
there were 42 PSEs  in Jammu and Kashmir under the audit jurisdiction of the CAG. 
These included 39 Government Companies, two Statutory Corporations 1 and  
one Government Controlled Other Company 2. The 39 Government Companies 
included six 3 inactive companies (including four under liquidation).  One Company, 
Jammu & Kashmir Bank Limited, is listed on the National Stock Exchange and 
Bombay Stock Exchange. Eleven PSEs earned profit of ` 1,286.79 crore and 20 PSEs 
incurred loss of ` 252.53 crore as per the latest finalised accounts as of September 2023. 
1.2  Accountability Framework 
The procedures for audit of Government Companies are laid down in Section 139 and 
143 of the Companies Act, 2013 (Act 2013). According  to Section 2 (45) of the Act 
2013, a Government Company means any Company in which not less than 51 per cent 
of the paid -up share capital is  held by the Central Government or by any State 
Government or Governments or partly by the Central Government and partly by one or 
more State Governments and includes a Company which is a subsidiary  Company of 
such a Government Company. The Comptroller and Auditor  General of India (CAG) 
appoints the statutory auditors of a Government Company and Government controlled 
other Company under Section 139 (5)  and (7) of the Companies Act, 2013.  
Section 139 (5) of the Companies Act, 2013 provides that the Statutory Auditors in case 
of a Government  Company or Government controlled other Company are to be 
appointed by the CAG within a period of 180 days from the  commencement of the 
financial year. Section 139 (7) of the Companies  Act, 2013 provides that in case of a 
 
1 Jammu and Kashmir Road Transport Corporation and Jammu and Kasmir and Ladakh Financial Corporation. 
2 Chenab Valley Power Projects (Private) Limited ( CVPPPL), a joint venture of Jammu and Kashmir Power 
Development Corporation (JKPDC), National Hydroelectric Power Corporation (NHPC) and  Power Trading 
Corporation (PTC). 
3 (1) Tawi Scooters Limited (2) Himalayan Wool Combers Limited (3) Jammu & Kashmir Handloom Handicrafts 
Raw Material Supplies Organisation Limited (A Subsidiary of Himalayan Wool Combers Limited) (4) Jammu 
& Kashmir Road Development Corporation Limited (5) Jammu & Kashmir International Trade Centre and  
(6) Jammu & Kashmir Asset Reconstruction Limited. 

Composite Audit Report (Civil and PSEs) for the period ended March 2023 
 
2 
 
Government Company or Government controlled other Company, the first auditor is to 
be appointed by the CAG within 60 days from the date of registration of the Company 
and in case the CAG does not appoint such auditor within the said period, the Board of 
Directors of the Company or the members of the Company have to appoint such auditor. 
Further, as per sub-section 7 of Section 143 of the Act 2013, the CAG  may, in case of 
any Company covered under sub -section (5) or sub -section (7) of Section 139, if 
considered necessary, by an order, cause test audit to  be conducted of the accounts of 
such Company and the provisions of  Section 19A of the Comptroller and Auditor 
General’s (Duties, Power and Conditions of Service) Act, 1971 shall apply to the report 
of such test  Audit. Thus, a Government Company or any other Company owned or  
controlled, directly or indirectly, by the Central Government or by any  State 
Government or Governments or partly by Central Government and  partly by one or 
more State Governments is subject to audit by the CAG. 
Statutory Audit 
The financial statements of the Government Companies (as defined  in Section 2 (45) 
of the Act 2013) are audited by Statutory Auditors, who  are appointed by the CAG as 
per the provisions of Section 139 (5) or (7) of  the Act 2013. The Statutory Auditors 
submit a copy of the Audit Report to the CAG including, among other things, financial 
statements of the  Company under Section 143(5) of the Act 2013. These financial  
statements are also subject to supplementary audit by the CAG within 60 days from the 
date of receipt of the audit report under the provisions  of Section 143 (6) of the Act 
2013. Audit of Statutory Corporations is governed by their respective legislations. Out 
of two Statutory Corporations, the CAG is the sole Auditor for Jammu and Kashmir 
Road Transport Corporation. In respect of Jammu and Kashmir and Ladakh Financial 
Corporation, the audit is conducted by Chartered Accountants and supplementary audit 
is conducted by the CAG. 
1.3  Submission of Accounts by UTPSEs 
Need for timely finalisation and submission of accounts 
According to Section 394 and 395 of the Companies Act, 2013,  Annual Report on the 
working and affairs of a Government Company, is to  be prepared within three months 
of its Annual General Meeting (AGM)  and as soon as may be after such preparation 
laid before the House or both the Houses of State/UT Legislature together with a copy 
of the Audit Report and any comments upon or supplement to the Audit Report, made 
by the CAG. Almost similar provisions exist in the respective Acts regulating statutory 
corporations. This mechanism provides the necessary legislative  control over the 
utilisation of public funds invested in the Companies from the Consolidated Fund of 
the State/UT. Section 96 of the Companies Act, 2013 requires every Company to hold 
AGM of the shareholders once in every  calendar year and that AGM is held within  
15 months of the previous/ last AGM. 

Chapter-I: Functioning of UT Public Sector Enterprises 
 
3 
 
Further, Section 129 of the Companies Act, 2013 stipulates that the  audited Financial 
Statement for the concerned financial year has to be  placed in the said AGM for their 
consideration. Section 129 (7) of the Companies Act, 2013 provides for levy of penalty 
like fine and  imprisonment on the persons including Directors of the Company 
responsible for non-compliance with the provisions of Section 129 of the  Companies 
Act, 2013. 
Role of Government and Legislature  
The State/UT Government exercises control over the affairs of these  PSEs through its 
administrative departments. The Chief Executive and  Directors to the Board are 
appointed by the State /UT Government. The State/UT Legislature also monitors the 
accounting and utilisation of Government investment in the PSEs. For this, the Annual 
Reports together with the Statutory Auditors’ Reports and comments of the CAG,  in 
respect of State /UT Government Companies and Separate Audit Reports in  case of 
Statutory Corporations are to be placed before the State /UT Legislature under Section 
394 of the Act, 2013 or as stipulated in the respective Acts.  The Audit Reports of the 
CAG are submitted to the Government for  placing before the State /UT Legislature 
under the provisions of Section 19A of  the CAG’s (Duties, Power and Conditions of 
Service) Act, 1971. 
1.4  Investment by GoJ&K in UTPSEs 
The UT Government of J&K (GoJ&K) has significant financial stake  in the PSEs. 
These are mainly of three types: 
Share capital and loans – In addition to the share capital  contribution, GoJ&K also 
provides financial assistance by way of loans to the PSEs from time to time. 
Special financial support – GoJ&K provides budgetary support by way of grants and 
subsidies to the PSEs as and when required. 
Guarantees – GoJ&K also guarantees the repayment of loans with interest availed by 
the PSEs from Financial Institutions. 
The thrust of PSE investment was on the power sector. The power sector had attracted 
77.58 per cent (` 29,879.63 crore) of total investment of ` 38,516.67 crore. The GoJ&K 
had invested 46.84  per cent (` 2,593.54 crore) of its total investment of  
` 5,536.96 crore in power sector PSEs. 
This Part of the Report further contains Chapters on: 
Chapter II: Financial Performance of UT Public Sector Enterprises 
Chapter III: Oversight Role of CAG 
Chapter IV: Corporate Governance 
Chapter V: Corporate Social Responsibility

2.1  Introduction 
As on 31 March 2023, there were 42 PSEs  in Jammu & Kashmir under the audit 
jurisdiction of the CAG. These included 39 Government Companies, two Statutory 
Corporations and one Government Controlled Other Company1(Appendix 2.1). 
The 39 Government Companies included six2 inactive companies (including four under 
liquidation). The Government of Jammu and Kashmir  and others had investment of  
` 58.40 crore in these inactive companies (Capital: ` 57.57 crore and Long-Term Loans: 
` 0.83 crore). This is a critical area and the Government may like to take an early 
decision to wind up the inactive PSEs as they do not contribute to the economic growth. 
One Company, Jammu & Kashmir Bank Limited, is listed on the National Stock 
Exchange and Bombay Stock Exchange. 
The ratio of turnover of the PSEs to the Gross State Domestic Product (GSDP) of J&K 
indicates the contribution of their activities in the economy of J&K. The details of 
turnover of PSEs are given in Appendix 2.2.

of three years ending March 2023.

2.4.2 Interest Coverage in PSEs  
Interest Coverage Ratio (ICR) is used to determine the ability of a Company to pay 
interest on outstanding debt and is calculated by dividing a Company's Earnings Before 
Interest and Taxes (EBIT) by interest expenses of the same period. The lower the ratio, 
the lesser the ability of the Company to pay interest on debt. An ICR of below one 
indicates that the Company was not generating sufficient revenues to meet its expenses 
on interest.

Limited for clearing overdues on account of power purchased and transmission charges; out of which loan

during 2022-23 respectively.

2.5.3  Return on Capital Employed and Equity in Listed PSE 
The profitability of a Company is traditionally assessed through return on equity and 
return on capital employed. Return on Capital Employed (ROCE) 14 is a financial ratio 
that measures the Company’s profitability and the efficiency with which its capital is 
used and is calculated by dividing the Company’s earnings before interest and taxes by 
capital employed. Return on Equity (ROE)15 is a measure of performance calculated by 
dividing the net profit after tax by shareholders’ funds. 
Details of ROCE and ROE of Jammu & Kashmir Bank Limited, the only listed 
Company, is given in Table 2.12: 
Table 2.12: ROCE and ROE of Listed PSE 
(in per cent) 
  2020-21 2021-22 2022-23 
ROCE 15.50 14.56 24.07 
ROE 21.00 20.47 37.95 
The ROCE and ROE was high due to high Profit After Tax earned by the Jammu and 
Kashmir Bank Limited.  
The ROCE worked out at realised value16 of investment in respect of Jammu & Kashmir 
Bank Limited is given in Table 2.13. 
 
 
13 ROI= (Government’s share of the Market Capitalisation of the Company as at 31 March of FY + Present Value 
of Dividend Receipts of the Government as at 31 March of FY + Present Value of Disinvestment Receipts of the 
Government as at 31 March of FY) – (Paid-up Equity of the Government as at Inception + Discounted Value of 
Equity infused by the Government as at Inception + Discounted Value of Subsidy/Grants infused for meeting 
Operational and Administrative Expenditure as at Inception)/ (Paid-up Equity of the Government as at Inception 
+ Discounted Value of Equity infused by the Government as at Inception + Discounted Value of Subsidy/ Grants 
infused for meeting Operational and Administrative Expenditure as at Inception)/ Number of intervening annual 
periods.

2.5.5  Rate of real return (RORR) of Government Investment in PSEs 
In order to bring the historical cost of investment to its present value at the end of each 
year upto 31 March 2023, Present Value (PV) of money invested was computed where 
funds had been infused by GoJ&K  as equity, interest free loan and interest free loan 
converted into equity. Grant/ subsidy given by the Government for operational and 
management expenses have not been considered as bifurcation of grant and subsidy for 
operational and management expenses and for other purpose was not available since 
inception of these companies till 31 March 2023. 
The PV in these undertakings was computed on the following assumptions: 
• Interest free loans have been considered as fund infusion. However, in case of 
repayment of loans by the PSEs, the PV was calculated on the reduced balances 
of interest free loans over the period.  
• The average rate of interest on government borrowings for the concerned 
financial year was adopted as discount rate for arriving at Present Value since 
they represent the cost incurred by the Government towards investment of funds 
for the year. 
• GoJ&K infused ` 5.00 crore as equity in JKPDCL. In addition to this, the 
GoJ&K also provided Plan funds since inception of the Company (1994-95) for 
creation of Capital Assets, which could not be bifurcated, has not been 
considered. Plan fund given to JKPDD and Assets tra nsferred to JKPDC by 
 
17 Excluding CVPPPL as all its projects are under construction. RoCE is calculated after excluding EBIT and 
Capital Employed figures of those PSEs whose Capital Employed was negative. RoE is calculated after  
excluding PAT and Shareholders Fund figures of those PSEs whose Shareholders Fund was negative.

As on 31 March 2023, there were 19 PSEs with accumulated losses of  
` 4,241.34 crore  (Appendix 2.9). These 19 PSEs had reported losses amounting to  
` 250.51 crore in their latest finalised accounts. The net worth of 14 out of 19 PSEs had 
been completely eroded by their accumulated loss. Out of these 14 PSEs, the networth 
of one PSE was negative for the last one year, of two PSEs for two years, another one 
for three years and the remaining 10 PSEs for the last five or more than five years. The 
net worth of these 14 PSEs was  (-) ` 3,699.38 crore against equity investment of  
` 537.12 crore as on 31 March 2023. The details of these 14 PSEs are given in  
Appendix 2.10. 
Further as per latest finalised accounts of these 14 PSEs, against their total income of  
` 728.19 crore, the total expenditure was ` 976.9324 crore, resulting in gap of  
` 248.74 crore. The total borrowings of these PSEs stood at ` 2,560.26 crore at the end 
of that year.  
2.7  Conclusion 
Out of 42 UTPSEs in UT of Jammu and Kashmir including two Statutory corporations; 
six UTPSEs were inactive and 36 were active UTPSEs. The thrust of PSE investment 
was on power sector which attracted 78 per  cent of the total investment.  Eleven UT 
PSEs were profit making as per their latest finalized accounts and only one UTPSE had 
declared/ paid dividend.  Twenty UTPSEs had reported losses in their latest finalised 
accounts and net worth of 14 out of 19 PSEs had been completely er oded by their 
accumulated loss.

CHAPTER-III 
OVERSIGHT ROLE OF CAG 

19 
 
CHAPTER-III 
OVERSIGHT ROLE OF CAG 
3.1  Audit of Public Sector Enterprises (PSEs) 
Comptroller & Auditor General of India (CAG) appoints the statutory auditors of a 
State Government Company and State Government Controlled Other Company under 
Section 139 (5) and (7) of the Companies Act, 2013. CAG has a right to conduct a 
supplementary audit and issue comments upon or supplement the Audit Report of the 
statutory auditor. Statutes governing some Corporations require that their accounts be 
audited by the CAG and a report be submitted to the State Legislature. 
3.2  Appointment of statutory auditors of PSEs by CAG 
Section 139(5) of the Companies Act, 2013 provides that the statutory auditors in case 
of a Government Company are to be appointed by the CAG within a period of 180 days 
from the commencement of the financial year. 
3.3  Submission of accounts by PSEs 
3.3.1  Need for timely submission 
According to Section 394 of the Companies Act, 2013 Annual Report on the working 
and affairs of a Government Company, is to be prepared within three months of its 
Annual General Meeting (AGM) and as soon as may be after such preparation laid 
before both the Houses of Legislature together with a copy of the Audit Report and any 
comments upon or supplement to the Audit Report, made by the CAG. Almost similar 
provisions exist in the respective Acts regulating statutory corporations. This 
mechanism provides the  necessary legislative control over the utilisation of public 
funds invested in the companies from the Consolidated Fund of the State/UT. 
Section 96 of the Companies Act, 2013 requires every Company to hold AGM of the 
shareholders once in every calendar year. It is also stated that not more than 15 months 
shall elapse between the date of one AGM and that of the next. Further, Section 129 of 
the Companies Act, 2013 stipulates that the audited Financial Statement for the 
financial year has to be placed in the said AGM for their consideration by the 
shareholders. 
Section 129 (7) of the Companies Act, 2013 also provides for levy of penalty like fine 
and imprisonment on persons including Directors of the Company responsible for 
non-compliance with the provisions of Section 129 of the Companies Act, 2013.  
Despite the above, annual accounts of various PSEs were pending as on 
30 September 2023, as detailed in the following paragraph. 
 
 

Composite Audit Report (Civil and PSEs) for the period ended March 2023

During the exit conference (April 2024), the Departmental representatives assured that 
all the PSEs shall be directed to comply with the statutory provisions regarding 
submission of accounts. 
3.4  CAG’s oversight - Audit of accounts and supplementary audit 
3.4.1  Financial reporting framework 
Companies are required to prepare the financial statements in the format laid down in 
Schedule III to the Companies Act, 2013 and in adherence to the mandatory Accounting 
Standards prescribed by the Central Government,  in consultation with National 
Advisory Committee on Accounting Standards. The statutory corporations are required 
to prepare their accounts in the format prescribed under the rules, framed in consultation 
with the CAG and any other specific provision relating to accounts in the Act governing 
such corporations. 
 

Composite Audit Report (Civil and PSEs) for the period ended March 2023 
 
22 
 
3.4.2  Audit of accounts of Government Companies by Statutory Auditors 
The statutory auditors appointed by the CAG under Section 139 of the Companies Act, 
2013 conduct audit of accounts of the Government Companies and submit their reports 
thereon in accordance with Section 143 of the Companies Act, 2013. 
The CAG plays an oversight role by monitoring the performance of the statutory 
auditors in audit of public sector undertakings with the overall objective that the 
statutory auditors discharge the functions assigned to them properly and effectively. 
This function is discharged by exercising the power: 
• to issue directions to the statutory auditors under Section 143 (5) of the 
Companies Act, 2013 and 
• to supplement or comment upon the statutory auditor's report under  
Section 143 (6) of the Companies Act, 2013. 
3.4.3  Supplementary Audit of accounts of Government Companies 
The responsibility for preparation of financial statements in accordance with the 
financial reporting framework prescribed under the Companies Act, 2013 or other 
relevant Act is of the management of an entity. 
The statutory auditors appointed by the CAG under Section 139 of the Companies Act, 
2013 are responsible for expressing an opinion on the financial statements under 
Section 143 of the Companies Act, 2013 based on independent audit in accordance with 
the Standard Auditing Practices of Institute of Chartered Accountants of India (ICAI) 
and directions given by the CAG. The statutory auditors are required to submit the 
Audit Report to the CAG under Section 143 of the Companies Act, 2013. 
The certified accounts of selected Government Companies along with the report of the 
statutory auditors are reviewed by CAG by carrying out a supplementary audit. Based 
on such review, significant audit observations, if any, are reported under Section  
143 (6) of the Companies Act, 2013 to be placed before the Annual General Meeting. 
3.5  Result of CAG’s oversight role 
Position of supplementary audit of the financial statements of the PSEs conducted by 
the Comptroller and Auditor General (CAG) of India for the year 2022-23 (or of earlier 
years which were finalised during the current year) is discussed in the next paragraph.

3.6  Conclusion 
Out of 34 UTPSEs in UT of Jammu and Kashmir for which accounts for 2022-23 were

by 30 September 2023 . Nineteen UTPSEs are those with accounts in arrears for

Kashmir Road Transport Corporation and Jammu & Kashmir and Ladakh Financial 
Corporation were in arrears for two years and more as on 30 September 2023. 
3.7  Recommendation 
• The Government may impress upon those PSEs with arrear in accounts to  
ensure early finalisation of their Financial Statements as in the absence of 
their finalisation, Government investments in such PSEs remain outside the 
legislative oversight. 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
CHAPTER-IV 
CORPORATE GOVERNANCE 
  

25 
 
CHAPTER-IV 
CORPORATE GOVERNANCE 
4.1  Corporate Governance  
Corporate Governance generally refers to practices by which organisations are directed, 
controlled and held to account. The absence of good governance structure or the  
non-adherence to the governance principles would increase the risk of public corruption 
and misuse of entrusted power by the management in the public sector. 
4.1.1  Provisions as contained in the Companies Act, 2013 
The Companies Act, 2013 was enacted on 29 August 2013 replacing the Companies 
Act, 1956. In addition, the Ministry of Corporate Affairs has also notified  
(31 March 2014) Companies Rules, 2014 on Appointment and Qualification of 
Directors, Meetings of Board and its powers and Accounts. The Companies Act, 2013 
together with the Companies Rules provide a robust framework for Corporate 
Governance. The requirements inter-alia provide for:  
• Qualifications for Independent  Directors along with the duties and guidelines 
for professional conduct (Section 149(6) of the Companies Act, 2013 read with 
Rule 5 of the Companies (Appointment and Qualification of Directors) 
Rules, 2014. 
• Mandatory appointment of at least one-Woman Director on the Board. Section 
149 (1) of the Companies Act, 2013, read with Rule 3 of the Companies 
(Appointment and Qualification of Directors) Rules 2014, provides that every 
listed Company and every other Pub lic Company having paid up share capital 
of ` 100 crore or more; or turnover of ` 300 crore or more shall appoint at least 
one woman Director on its Board. 
• Mandatory establishment of Audit Committee (Section 177(1) of Companies 
Act, 2013) and Nomination and Remuneration Committee (Section 178(1) of 
Companies Act, 2013). 
• Holding of a minimum of four meetings of Board of Directors every year in 
such a manner that not more than 120 days shall intervene between  
two consecutive meetings of the Board {Section 173(1)}. 
4.1.2  SEBI’s guidelines on Corporate Governance for listed companies 
With the enactment of Companies Act, 2013, Securities and Exchange Board of India 
(SEBI) amended (April and September 2014) Clause 49 of the Listing Agreement to 
align it with the Corporate Governance provisions specified in the Companies 
Act, 2013.  

Composite Audit Report (Civil and PSEs) for the period ended March 2023 
 
26 
 
SEBI, further issued (13 October 2015) a uniform listing agreement format for all types 
of securities which required the listed entity to comply with the provisions of SEBI 
(Listing Obligations and Disclosure Requirements) Regulations, 2015.  
4.2  Scope of Audit 
As on 31 March 2023, there were 42 UT Public Sector Enterprises (UTPSEs) in Jammu 
and Kashmir under the audit jurisdiction of the CAG. These included 39 Government 
Companies (Public Limited: 21; Private Limited: 18), one Government controlled other 
Company1) and two Statutory Corporations. 
The 39 Government Companies included six 2 inactive/ non -operational Companies. 
One Company, Jammu and Kashmir Bank Limited, is listed on the National Stock 
Exchange and Bombay Stock Exchange.  
For corporate Governance, an assessment framework was prepared based on the 
provisions contained in the Companies Act 2013. Guidelines/ regulations issued by 
SEBI (April and September 2014) were also reflected in the assessment framework. 
Out of 40 companies, the review covers 33 active Government companies  
(Public Limited: 17 including one listed Company3, Private Limited: 15 and one J&K 
Government controlled other Company) for the year ended 31 March 2023. Six inactive 
companies and one Public Limited Company viz. J&K Cement Limited, being  
non-operational, have not been covered.  
4.3  Composition of Board of Directors 
Section 2 (10) of the Companies Act, 2013 defined that “Board of Directors” (BoDs) 
or “Board”, in relation to a Company, means the collective body of the Directors of the 
Company. 
4.3.1  Independent Directors on the Board 
As per Regulation 17(1)(b) of SEBI (Listing Obligations and Disclosure Requirements) 
Regulations, 2015, at least half of the BoDs of the listed entity shall consist of 
Independent Directors. Further, Section 149(4) of the Companies Act, 2013 prescribes 
that every listed Public Company is mandatorily required to have at least one -third of 
the total number of Directors as Independent Directors (IDs).  
 
1 Chenab Valley Power Projects (Private) Limited (CVPPPL), a joint venture of Jammu and Kashmir State Power 
Development Corporation Limited, Power Trading Corporation and National Hydroelectric Power Corporation 
Limited. 
2 Inactive companies: (1) Tawi Scooters Limited (2) Himalayan Wool Combers Limited, (3) Jammu &  Kashmir

Limited), (4) Jammu & Kashmir Road Development Corporation Limited, (5) Jammu & Kashmir International 
Trade Centre, (6) Jammu & Kashmir Asset Reconstruction Limited; Non-operational Company: (6) J&K Cement 
Limited. 
3 Jammu & Kashmir Bank Limited. 

Chapter-IV: Corporate Governance 
27 
 
Moreover, Rule 4 of the Companies (Appointment and Qualification of Directors) 
Rules, 2014, stipulates that unlisted public companies must appoint at least  
two Independent Directors in the following circumstances: 
i. If the paid-up share capital is ` 10 crore or more; or 
ii. if the turnover is ` 100 crore or more; or 
iii. if the aggregate of all the outstanding loans, debentures and deposits exceeds  
` 50 crore. 
Audit noted that out of 17 Public Limited UTPSEs, one listed Public Company Jammu 
and Kashmir Bank Limited falls under the purview of Regulation 17(1) (b) of SEBI 
(Listing Obligations and Disclosure Requirements) Regulations, 2015 whereas  
nine UTPSEs were governed by the provisions of Section 149 (4) of the Companies 
Act, 2013, as per details given in Appendix 4.1. However, only one UTPSE had 
appointed Independent Directors as detailed in Table 4.1.

4.3.2  Woman Director on the Board 
Section 149 (1) of the Companies Act, 2013, read with Rule 3 of the Companies 
(Appointment and Qualification of Directors) Rules 2014, provides that every listed 
Company and every other Public Company having paid-up share capital of ` 100 crore 
or more; or turnover of ` 300 crore or more shall appoint at least one-Woman Director 
on its Board. 
As per the requirement of the aforesaid provisions, Woman Directors were to be 
appointed in one Public Limited UTPSE as mentioned in Table 4.2.

4.5  Audit Committee 
4.5.1  Formation of Audit Committee  
Section 177 of the Act read with Rule 6 of the Companies (Meetings of the Board and 
Powers) Rules, 2014 stipulates that the Board of Directors of every listed Company and 
the following classes of Companies are required to constitute an Audit Committee of 
the Board: 
• All Public Companies with a paid-up capital of ` 10 crore or more; 
• All Public Companies having turnover of ` 100 crore rupees or more; 
• All Public Companies, having in aggregate, outstanding Loans or Borrowings 
or Debentures or Deposits exceeding ` 50 crore or more. 
Audit observed that out of 17 Public Limited UTPSEs, 10 UTPSEs were required to 
constitute an Audit Committee  (Appendix 4.1), however, only one Company viz. 
Jammu & Kashmir Bank Limited had constituted Audit Committee during 2022-23.

Section 178 (1) of the Companies Act, 2013read with Rule 6 of the Companies 
(Meeting of Boards and its Powers) Rules, 2014 provides that the following classes of 
Companies are required to constitute a Nomination and Remuneration Committee:  
• All Public Companies with a paid-up capital of ` 10 crore rupees or more; 
• all Public Companies having turnover of ` 100 crore rupees or more; 
• all Public Companies, having in aggregate, outstanding Loans or Borrowings or 
Debentures or Deposits exceeding ` 50 crore or more. 
Audit observed that out of 17 Public Limited Companies,10 Companies were required 
to constitute a Nomination and Remuneration Committee  (Appendix 4.1). However, 
only one Company viz. Jammu &  Kashmir Bank Limited had constituted Nomination 
and Remuneration Committee during 2022-23.

4.8  Conclusion 
Audit reviewed 33 UTPSEs and observed that although ten UTPSEs were eligible to 
appoint Independent Directors, only one UTPSE (J&K Bank Limited) had appointed 
the required number of Independent Directors during 2022-23. Out of 33 UTPSEs, ten 
UTPSEs were eligible to constitute an Audit Committee and Nomination and 
Remuneration Committee, however, only one UTPSE, J&K Bank limited  had formed 
such committees. Only two out of eight eligible UTPSEs had appointed a whole time 
Company Secretary, in one UTPSE, the work of Company Secretary was being carried 
out by part -time Company Secretary and in remaining five UTPSEs, no Company 
Secretary had been appointed. Only nine out of 33 UTPSEs had conducted the required 
number of BoD meetings during 2022 -23. AG M was not conducted in 24 out of 
33UTPSEs, during 2022-23. 
4.9  Recommendation 
The Government may: 
• The Government of Jammu and Kashmir may direct the respective 
Administrative Departments to ensure compliance with the Companies Act 
2013 to achieve the objectives of Corporate Governance in UTPSEs. The 
Board of Directors of the respective UTPSEs may  also ensure compliance 
with the relevant provisions of the Companies Act 2013. 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
CHAPTER-V 
CORPORATE SOCIAL 
RESPONSIBILITY 
  

 
33 
 
CHAPTER-V 
CORPORATE SOCIAL RESPONSIBILITY 
5.1  Introduction 
Corporate Social Responsibility (CSR) is the continuing commitment by business to 
behave ethically and contribute to economic development while improving the quality 
of life of the local community at large. It recognises the interests of its stakeholders and 
the general community at large by covering sustainability, social impact and ethics. By 
performing the task of CSR activities, the Companies are giving something back to the 
society. The inclusion of CSR mandate under the Companies Act, 2013 is an attem pt 
to supplement the Government's efforts of equitably delivering the benefits of growth 
and engage the corporate world with country's development agenda. 
Legal Framework: Section 135 of the Companies Act, 2013 (Act) deals with the 
subject of Corporate Social Responsibility and lays down the qualifying criteria based 
on net worth, turnover and net profit during immediately preceding financial year for 
companies which are req uired to undertake CSR activities and specifies the broad 
modalities of selection, implementation and monitoring of the CSR activities by the 
Board of Directors of the Company.  The activities which may be included by the 
Companies in their CSR policies are listed in Schedule VII of the Act. The provisions 
of Section 135 of the Act and Schedule VII of the Act are applicable to all companies 
including UT Public Sector Enterprises (UTPSEs). 
The Act makes it mandatory for any Company to spend, annually at least two per cent 
of average net profit 1 of three immediately preceding financial years towards CSR 
activities. In February 2014, Ministry of Corporate Affairs (MCA) issued Companies 
(Corporate Social Responsibility Policy) Rules, 2014 made applicable to all companies 
including UTPSEs w.e.f. 1 April 2014. Accordingly, the compliance of the provisions 
of CSR under the Act i.e. constitution of CSR Committee, formulation of CSR Policy 
and spending of prescribed amount on CSR activities came into force from  
01 April 2014. 
As per criterion specified under Section 135 (1) of the Act, out of 33 active UTPSEs in 
2022-23, nine UTPSEs (Appendix 5.1) met the criteria re quired to undertake CSR 
activities. It was seen that the CSR activities were carried out by three UTPSEs 
(Appendix 5.3) during the year 2022 -23. All of these three UTPSEs 2 had provided 
budget for undertaking CSR activities during the year 2022-23. 
 
 
 
1 Calculated as per Section 198 of the Companies Act, 2013. 
2 Jammu and Kashmir Bank Limited, Jammu and Kashmir State Power Development Corporation Limited  and 
Chenab Valley Power Projects Private Limited. 

Composite Audit Report (Civil and PSEs) for the period ended March 2023 
 
34 
 
5.2  Audit findings 
Audit findings on extent of compliance with the provisions of the Act with regard to 
constitution of CSR Committee; formulation and compliance of policy; planning and 
execution of CSR activities; and monitoring and reporting thereof by the UTPSEs are 
given in the succeeding paragraphs. 
Planning 
5.2.1  Constitution of CSR Committee 
As per Section 135 (1) of the Act, every company having a net worth of ` 500 crore or 
more; or turnover of ` 1,000 core or more; or a net profit of ` 5 crore or more during 
immediately preceding financial year shall constitute a CSR committee of the Board of 
Directors (BoDs).  
It was seen that out of nine UTPSEs, only four UTPSEs 3 had constituted the CSR 
committee for undertaking CSR activities during 2022-23. 
5.2.2  Independent Directors in Committee 
As per Section 135 (1) of the Act, the CSR committee shall have at least one 
Independent Director 4. Out of the four UTPSEs which had formulated the CSR 
committee, two UTPSEs (Chenab Valley Power Project Private Limited and Jammu 
and Kashmir State Power Development Corporation Limited) were not required to 
appoint an Independent Director, one UTPSE (Jammu and Kashmir Bank Limited) had 
complied with the requirement of having at least one Independent Director in the 
committee and other one UTPSE (Jammu and Kashmir Police Housing Corporation ) 
did not comply with the requirement of having Independent Director in its CSR 
committee during 2022-23. 
5.2.3  Framing of CSR policy 
Section 135 (3) of the Act requires that Company’s CSR Committee shall formulate 
and recommend to the Board, a CSR Policy. It was seen that out of nine UTPSEs,  
four UTPSEs framed their CSR policy on recommendation of the CSR Committee 
while the remaining five UTPSEs5 did not have any CSR Policy in place. 
5.2.4  Annual CSR Plan and Budget 
Role of the CSR Committee is to recommend to the Board, the CSR activities and the 
amount to be spent in the financial year. The Board has to ensure implementation of the 
 
3 (1) Jammu and Kashmir Bank Limited (2) Chenab Valley Power Project Private Limited (3) Jammu and Kashmir 
State Power Development Corporation Limited and (4) Jammu and Kashmir Police Housing Corporation 
Limited. 
4 Provided that where a company is not required to appoint an independent director under Section 149(4), it shall 
have in its CSR committee two or more directors. 
5 (1) Jammu and Kashmir Cable Car Corporation Limited (2) Jammu and Kashmir Forest Development 
Corporation Limited (3) Jammu and Kashmir Medical Supplies Corporation Limited (4) Kashmir Power 
Distribution Corporation Limited and (5) Jammu Power Distribution Company Limited. 

Chapter-V: Corporate Social Responsibility 
35 
 
CSR activities. As a best practice, the expenditure on CSR activities is to be incurred 
evenly during the whole year so as to avoid rush of expenditure to exhaust the funds at 
the end of the year. 
It was seen that out of total expenditure of ` 3.05 crore incurred on CSR activities 
during 2022-23, ` 2.73 crore (89 per cent) was incurred in 4 th quarter of 2022 -23 by 
three UTPSEs. 
5.3  Financial component 
5.3.1  Allocation of funds and utilisation thereof 
Section 135 (5) of the Act makes it mandatory for any Company to spend, annually, at 
least two per cent of average net profit made during the three immediately preceding 
financial years. 
As worked out by Audit, out of nine UTPSEs; seven UTPSEs were required to incur 
expenditure on CSR activities to the extent of ` 12.64 crore (Appendix 5.2). Remaining 
two UTPSEs were not required to incur expenditure on CSR activities due to the reasons 
that these two UTPSEs 6 had incurred losses during the three immediately preceding 
financial years. 
Audit noticed that only three UT PSEs (out of seven) incurred expenditure on CSR 
activities aggregating to ` 3.05 crore, however, allocation of ` 27.42 crore (including  
` 16.34 crore of previous years brought forwarded) was made (Appendix 5.3).  
Against the required spending of ` 27.42 crore by three UTPSEs, the actual spending 
was ` 3.05 crore leaving unspent amount of ` 24.37 crore by two UTPSEs  
(Appendix 5.3). Jammu and Kashmir Bank Limited had fully utilised the CSR funds 
allocated during 2022-23. Against allocated funds of ` 25.80 crore by the Jammu and 
Kashmir State Power Development Corporation Limited , expenditure of ` 1.57 crore 
only was incurred i.e. shortfall of ` 24.23 crore (94 per cent). Similarly, against the 
allocated funds of ` 0.44 crore by Chenab Valley Power Projects Private Limited , 
expenditure of ` 0.30 crore only was incurred i.e. there was a shortfall of ` 0.14 crore 
(32 per cent). 
As per Section 135(5) of the Act, if the company is unable to spend such amount, unless 
the unspent amount relates to any ongoing project, it needs to  transfer such unspent 
amount to a Fund specified in Schedule VII, within a period of six months of the expiry 
of the financial years.  
It was seen that out of total unspent amount of ` 24.23 crore in Jammu and Kashmir 
State Power Development Corporation Limited (Appendix 5.3), the unspent amount 
(not related to ongoing project) of  ` 7.60 crore of 2020 -21, ` 8.54 crore of 2021 -22  
and ` 8.09 crore of 2022 -23 was transferred in October 2024 (for 2020 -21 and  
2021-22) and in June 2025 (for 2022 -23) to Swachh Bharat Kosh (a fund specified in 
 
6 (1) Kashmir Power Distribution Corporation Limited and (2) Jammu Power Distribution Company Limited. 

Composite Audit Report (Civil and PSEs) for the period ended March 2023 
 
36 
 
Schedule VII) after a delay of three years, two years; and one year and nine months, 
respectively. 
5.4  Monitoring framework 
As per Rule 8 (3) of the Companies (Corporate Social Responsibility Policy) 
Rules, 2014, every company having average CSR obligation of ` 10 crore or more in 
pursuance of the Section 135 (5) of the Act, in the three immediately preceding financial 
years, shall undertake impact assessment, through an independent agency, of their CSR 
projects having outlays of one crore rupees or more, and wh ich have been completed 
and not less than one year before undertaking the impact study.  
It was seen that all four UTPSEs 7, which had framed CSR policy had specified the 
monitoring mechanism in the policy. 
It was further seen that the rule to carry out impact assessment through an independent 
agency for spending on CSR project/ activity was not applicable to any of the UTPSEs 
during 2022 -23 as average CSR obligation was less than ` 10 crore in the  
three immediately preceding financial years. 
5.5  Reporting and disclosure 
As per Rule 9 of the Companies (Corporate Social Responsibility Policy) Rules, 2014, 
the BoDs shall mandatorily disclose the composition of the CSR committee and CSR 
Policy and Projects approved by the Board on their website, if, any, for public access. 
The Companies have to disclose the following in the prescribed format: 
• Disclose contents of CSR policy, web link of CSR policy, average net profit, 
composition of CSR Committee, administrative overheads, prescribed amount, 
unspent amount, reasons for unspent amount. 
• Include a responsibility statement signed by the CSR Committee that the 
implementation and monitoring of CSR Policy was in compliance with the CSR 
objective and Policy of the Company. 
It was seen that out of three UTPSEs 8; compliance of above provisions was done by 
two UTPSEs (Jammu and Kashmir Bank Limited and Chenab Valley Power Projects 
Private Limited). 
5.6  Conclusion 
Out of nine UTPSEs in UT of Jammu and Kashmir, which met the criteria required to 
undertake CSR activities as per the Act, only four UTPSEs has framed their CSR policy. 
 
7 (1) Chenab Valley Power Projects Private Limited (2) Jammu and Kashmir State Power Development 
Corporation Limited (3) Jammu and Kashmir Bank Limited and (4) Jammu and Kashmir Police Housing 
Corporation Limited. 
8 (1) Chenab Valley Power Projects Private Limited (2)  Jammu and Kashmir State Power Development 
Corporation Limited and (3) Jammu and Kashmir Bank Limited. 

Chapter-V: Corporate Social Responsibility 
37 
 
Seven UTPSEs were required to incur expenditure during 2022-23, of which only three 
UTPSEs had incurred expenditure on CSR activities. 
5.7  Recommendations 
• All UTPSEs meeting the criteria may frame CSR Policy and constitute CSR 
Committee in compliance of provisions of the Companies Act, 2013 and CSR 
Rules.  
• UTPSEs required to undertake CSR activities may ensure requisite 
allocations are made  and utilised as per provisions of the Companies 
Act, 2013.

PA ON EXECUTION OF WORKS 
UNDER NABARD-RIDF BY PUBLIC

CHAPTER-VI 
PERFORMANCE AUDIT ON EXECUTION OF WORKS UNDER 
NABARD-RIDF BY PUBLIC WORKS AND JAL SHAKTI DEPARTMENTS 
6.1  Finance Department 
6.1.1  Introduction 
The Government of India (GoI) introduced the Rural Infrastructure Development Fund 
(RIDF) in 1995 -96 under the National Bank for Agriculture and Rural Development 
(NABARD) to provide funds for projects initiated but left incomplete due to inadequate 
resources of State/Union Territory Governments. The main objective of NABARD 
funding is to provide low -cost funding to Indian State Governments for completing 
delayed rural infrastructure projects, bridging the rural urban gap and promoting 
inclusive growth, cov ering areas like irrigation, roads, health, education and water. 
RIDF loans are available up to 95 per cent of project cost for agriculture and allied 
activities, and up to 90 per cent of project cost for social sector and rural connectivity 
projects in hilly and north-eastern States. RIDF follows a project-based lending model. 
Implementing Departments of the Government of Jammu and Kashmir (GoJ&K) 
submit Detailed Project Reports (DPRs),  containing technical and financial details, 
drawings and maps, to NABARD through the Finance Department, which is the nodal 
department. Eligible project proposals are considered for sanction by the NABARD 
sanctioning committee. After sanction, funds received from NABARD are released by 
the Finance Department to the Implementing Departments for execution. As of  
31 March 2023, a total of 28 RIDF tranches have been sanctioned for Jammu and 
Kashmir. 
6.1.2  Audit Objectives 
This Performance Audit (PA) was conducted to ascertain whether:  
➢ Planning process for selection of projects for funding under NABARD was 
adequate and effective; 
➢ Funds were effectively utilised and projects were executed efficiently and 
effectively with due regard to economy so that the intended benefits reached the 
targeted areas and population; 
➢ Internal controls and monitoring mechanisms were adequate and effective. 
6.1.3  Audit Criteria 
Audit objectives were benchmarked against the following criteria: 
➢ Handbook on Rural Infrastructure Development Fund (RIDF) issued by 
NABARD; 
➢ Jammu and Kashmir Financial Code 2006/ General Financial Rules 2017;  

Composite Audit Report (Civil and PSEs) for the period ended March 2023  
40 
 
➢ Jammu and Kashmir Public Works Account Code 2008/ Central Public Works 
Manual, 2022; 
➢ Jammu and Kashmir Engineering Manual (2020); 
➢ Rural Roads Manual of Indian Road Congress (IRC) Special Publications 20. 
6.1.4  Audit Scope, Sample and Methodology 
The Performance Audit covered the period from April 2018 to March 2023. Information 
and data were collected from NABARD Regional Office, Jammu. Records were 
examined in the Finance Department of GoJ&K (Nodal Department) and three selected 
executing Departm ents, namely Public Works Department (Roads and Buildings/  
R&B), Jal Shakti Department (Public Health Engineering/PHE) and Jal Shakti 
Department (Irrigation and Flood Control/I&FC). 
During 2018 -19 to 2022 -23, a total of 1,970 projects 1were implemented across all 
Departments, of which 1,746 projects pertained to the three selected Departments. Out 
of these, 700 projects (40 per cent)2 were selected for examination at the Apex Level  
(Chief Engineers and Finance Department, GoJ&K). Further, execution of 470 projects 
was examined in detail at Division Level across 16 districts 3 of J&K to ensure a 
geographically representative sample. Joint physical inspections were also conducted 
in selected districts along with Departmental authorities. 
An Entry Conference was held by the Principal Accountant General (Audit) with the 
Principal Secretary, Finance Department, GoJ&K in October 2023 to discuss the audit 
objectives, scope and methodology. An Exit Conference was held on 14 October 2025 
with the Jal Shakti Department (PHE and I&FC). The dates for holding Exit Conference 
were not confirmed by the Public Works (R&B) Department, despite repeated 
reminders4. However, reply was received from Public Works (R&B) Department on  
16 December 2025 and the replies have been suitably incorporated in this report. 
6.1.5  Status of Sanctioned Loans 
The borrowings of State/ UT Government under RIDF are governed by Article 293(3) 
of the Constitution of India. Under this article, Government of India (GoI) grants 
consent of borrowing to State/ UT Government for a financial year. Loans under RIDF 
projects are disbursed only after the State/ UT Government has obtained consent of 
borrowing from GoI. The position of consent of borrowings granted by GoI and loan 
availed by GoJ&K during 2018-19 to 2022-23 is given in Table 6.1.1.

6.1.6  Inordinate time lag in disbursal of Mobilisation Advance by Finance 
 Department 
For release of loans from NABARD, the Finance Department, GoJ&K submits a Time 
Promissory Note (TPN) 5 in the prescribed format to NABARD. After submission of 
TPN by the Finance Department, NABARD disburses Mobilisation Advance  (MA) 
within two to three days to Finance Department, GoJ&K.  
(i) There were time -lags in release of Mobilisation Advance by the Finance 
Department to the implementing departments, which ranged between 03 and 372 days6 
during 2018-19 to 2022-23. If the Finance Department maintained similar timelines for 
release as maintained by NABARD , GoJ&K could have avoided  interest burden of  
` 4.88 crore7 for the period during which the loan funds were retained by the Finance 
Department as detailed in Table 6.1.2. 
 
 
5 The Time promissory note is a formal document vide which the Lieutenant Governor of UT of J&K requests 
NABARD to release the loan and promises to pay NABARD the loan amount and interest on the loan on fix 
dates and at the fixed rate of interest. 
6 Time-lag was calculated after allowing three days since NABARD had released funds within two to three days 
to GoJ&K after submission of TPNs by the Finance Department. 
7 [(Amount held by Finance Department x Rate of Interest (Range: 2.75 per cent to 5.25 per cent) x Number of 
days held by Finance Department)/ 365)] x 100.

In case of 106 projects, there was time-lag of more than six months. Reasons for time-
lags were not evident from records. The Finance Department should have coordinated 
with the Departments to  avail the funds for MA (executed the TPN with NABARD) 
when these funds were likely to be released to implementing departments, as interest 
started accruing as soon as the loan is released by NABARD. 
Para 7.7 of the NABARD RIDF Handbook prescribes timeline for grounding of 
projects. However, Audit noted (February 2024) that the NABARD has not prescribed 
timelines for release of Mobilisation Advance by the Finance Department to respective 
Administrative Departments/ implementing units/ agencies of the Government due to 
which High-Powered Committee (HPC) could not take timely action.  
(ii)  In respect of 233 projects, the Finance Department did not release  
` 293.03 crore, received from NABARD during 2019-20 to 2022-23 (under RIDF-XXV 
to RIDF-XXVIII), to the Implementing Departments. As of March 2023, ` 293.03 crore 
had been retained by the Finance Department, GoJ&K which resulted in interest burden 
of ` 6.22 crore10. Besides, this resulted in delays in timely execution of the projects and 
the intended benefits of projects could not be derived timely. 
(iii)  As per the RIDF Handbook (2021), a project is to be treated as a non -starter if 
it is not started within 12 months from the date of issue of the sanction letter, 
irrespective of whether Mobilisation Advance (MA) has been availed. A project is 
considered as started only after issue of the work order and commencement of physical 
work. The entire MA released in respect of a non -starter project is required to be 
adjusted or recalled. 
Audit examination (February 2024) revealed that 68 out of 587 projects sanctioned 
during 2020-21 and 2021-22 under RIDF-XXVI and RIDF-XXVII had not been started 
as of March 2023, despite release of MA amounting to ` 96.42 crore. As execution of 
these projects had not commenced as of March 2023, the MA of ` 96.42 crore remained

Shakti Public Health Engineering Department (PHED). The Department wise findings

6.2  Public Works (R&B) Department 
As per J&K Economic Survey (2023), out of 41,140 km road length in J&K,  
38,710 km (94 per cent) were Rural/ Village/ District roads. Projects of Major District 
Roads, Other District Roads and Village roads are eligible for loan under NABARD 
funded Rural Infrastructure Development Fund (RIDF).  
Out of 1,350 projects 11 sanctioned during 2012 -13 to 2022 -23 under RIDF XVIII to 
XXVIII, Audit sampled 348 projects12 executed during 2018-23 in 24 Divisions across  
10 Districts. Of these, DPRs for only 310 projects were produced to Audit by the 
executing divisions. 
Further, execution of 309 projects in 24 Divisions and 10 Districts was test-checked for 
detailed scrutiny, as 39 projects could not be examined due to technical issues13. 
6.2.1  Planning for selection of projects 
Paragraph 2.1.4 of manual on RIDF provides that State/ UT Governments are expected 
to prioritize projects under a tranche and submit suitable proposals of eligible 
activities/projects with DPRs to NABARD for sanction. 
6.2.1.1  Execution of Works not based on Master Plan 
According to RIDF guidelines, norms specified by Indian Road Congress (IRC) have 
to be followed for appraisal of road projects. IRC -20 (Rural Roads Manual, 2002) 
envisages preparation of Master Plan for rural roads by building a comprehensive 
database for all habitations and existing networks of all types of roads and tracks. 
Further, Annexure –IV: Checklist for preparation of DPRs for loans under RIDF 
provides for preparation of State Master Plan and prioritisation of projects therein. The 
Master Plan data for rural road network should be collected/prepared at Block level and 
then integrated for a District level Master Plan. The Master Plan will provide rationality 
of choice and can save any wastage of resources. 
Audit check of records of 348 projects of all 24 sampled R&B Divisions 14 showed  
non-compliance of method prescribed in Para 7.4.1 of NABARD -RIDF Handbook as 
 
11 Jammu: 763 projects and Kashmir: 587 projects; Closed projects: 300 and Ongoing projects: 1,050. 
12 281 ongoing projects (Jammu: 148 projects and Kashmir: 133 projects) and 67 closed projects 
(Jammu: 38 projects and Kashmir: 29 projects). 
13 Under technical evaluation: 36, sanctioned twice: 2, and not traceable: 1. 
14 Jammu: 11 Divisions  (Construction Division -I Jammu, Construction Division -II Jammu, Construction 
Division-III Jammu, R&B Division Rajouri, R&B Division Nowshera, R&B Division Poonch, R&B Division 
Udhampur, Special Sub - Division Doda, R&B Division Bhaderwah, R&B Division Ramb an, R&B Division 
Kishtwar), Kashmir: 13 Divisions (R&B Division Khanabal. R&B Division Vailoo, R&B Division Qazigund, 
R&B Division Sopore, R&B Division Baramulla, R&B Division Uri, R&B Division Tangmarg, R&B Division 
Kupwara, R&B Division Handwara, R&B Division Tangdar, R&B Division Budgam, R&B Division Chadoora, 
and R&B Division Shopian. 

Chapter-VI: PA on Execution of Works under NABARD-RIDF by Public Works and Jal Shakti Departments 
45 
 
works were taken up randomly without preparation of Master Plans. Hence, execution 
of works not based on Master Plan affected the objective of developing optimal network 
of roads.  
In reply, the Department stated (December 2025) that the projects were primarily taken 
up on the basis of urgent public demand and recommendations received from the 
District Administration/ MLAs/ PRIs/ field staff. However, the mapping of roads and 
other infrastructure facilities is being carried out through the JKPWDOMS portal which 
will facilitate preparation of a comprehensive Master Plan and assist in planning and 
selection of projects.  
The reply does not address the reason for the works executed without the Master Plans 
during the period April 2018 to March 2023.  
6.2.2  Preparation of DPRs 
According to Para 7.3 of NABARD (RIDF) handbook, the DPRs are to be submitted 
for projects which are technically feasible and economically viable. Out of 348 sampled 
projects, DPRs of 38 projects were not available on records in nine test -checked 
divisions of seven Districts. It was further noticed that certain important norms were 
not followed in the selected 310 projects of 24 Divisions: 
(i) Benefit-Cost ratio: As per para 7.3 of Handbook on RIDF, the projects should 
be technically feasible and financially viable, and the Benefit-Cost-Ratio (BCR) should 
be more than one at a discounting factor of 10 per cent . In respect of DPRs of 
310 sampled projects, reliable data had not been obtained for calculation of BCR from 
Agriculture Production Department (agriculture production), Revenue Department 
(population, wage rates) and Department of Rural Development and Panchayati Raj 
Institutions (g eneration of additional employment). R&B Divisions had used 
approximated/ self-assessed figures which had no reliable basis. 
Executive Engineers of R&B Divisions accepted that BCR had been worked out based on 
approximate data of population, length of roads and operational cost of vehicles and 
stated that instructions have been noted for obtaining actual data from the concerned 
Departments. 
(ii) Earthquake zonal regulation : As per RIDF Manual, the project has to be 
designed as per earthquake zonal regulation stipulated in BIS code 15. A certificate to 
this effect is to be furnished by the  competent authority. In respect of 310 sampled 
projects, such certificates of the competent authority were not found on record. Though 
areas of Jammu and Kashmir fall under high seismic zone  (Zone IV or greater) , the 
critical aspect of structural safety was not duly considered prior to execution of the 
works.  
Executive Engineers, R&B Divisions noted the instructions for compliance. 
 
15 Bureau of Indian Standards. 

Composite Audit Report (Civil and PSEs) for the period ended March 2023  
46 
 
(iii) Geographical Information System (GIS) Maps : Paragraph 1.2.5 of IRC 
Special Publications 20 guidelines provide that digital maps with proper registration 
etc. should be prepared when master planning is done. Further, Para 7.1 of Handbook 
on RIDF provide that DPRs to be submitted for approval under NABARD should 
contain technical and financial parameters, drawings, maps, etc. by project 
implementing Departments.  It was noticed that 300 DPRs (97 per cent ) out of 
310 sampled projects16 did not contain requisite maps with GIS location. 
Executive Engineers of R&B Divisions stated (September 2023 to March 2024) that 
henceforth Global Positioning System (GPS) map would be attached in all DPRs. 
(iv) Length of multilink roads in projects : Appendix 1.4 of IRC (SP20.2002) 
envisages that total length of road has to be given accurately in the DPRs with the name 
of starting and ending habitation. Out of 310 sampled projects, multi-road links across 
various directions and locations were involve d in 33 projects. Out of these, in  
32 projects in 11 divisions of seven districts, the desired length of each road link was 
not specified in DPRs. The measurement of each link road could not be verified with 
the roads proposed, approved and constructed by Public Works (R&B) Department. As 
such the possibility of wrong inclusion/ exclusion of link roads could not be ruled out. 
Department in its reply stated (December 2025) that the projects taken up during the 
review period were based on preliminary feasibility assessment and urgent 
infrastructure needs, necessitated by connectivity gaps in remote areas. It was added 
that with t he introduction of GIS mapping in the Department, the process of project 
identification, formulation and monitoring has been significantly strengthened ensuring 
complete transparency and technical robustness. 
The reply of the Department is not acceptable as reliance on preliminary feasibility and 
urgent connectivity needs cannot substitute the mandatory requirement of preparing 
DPRs in accordance with RIDF and IRC guidelines. 
6.2.3  Sanction of Projects 
6.2.3.1  Slow progress due to non -compliance of obtaining pre -execution 
approvals 
Annexure IV of the Handbook of RIDF Guidelines prescribes checklist for clearances 
to be obtained from various authorities including the Ministry of Environment and 
Forests. Audit noticed certain deficiencies in planning of the following road projects 
before getting these sanctioned under NABARD loan.  
(I) The project “Construction of road from Lali Dabbar to Tote Phase-3” in Udhampur 
district was sanctioned in 2021 -22 under NABARD at a cost of ` 4.20 crore  
(Loan: ` 3.78 crore; State share: ` 0.42 crore). In the DPR of 3rd phase of the project, it 
was stated that the work of Phase -1 of the road project was in progress while forest 
 
16 Jammu: 180 projects; Kashmir: 130 projects. 

Chapter-VI: PA on Execution of Works under NABARD-RIDF by Public Works and Jal Shakti Departments 
47 
 
clearance was awaited for Phase-2 of the project. R&B Division Udhampur had allotted  
(September 2022) construction of Phase -2 of the road project to a contractor with a 
completion period of 24 months. 
Audit noticed (December 2023) that R&B Division Udhampur allotted  
(November 2022) construction of Phase -3 of the road project without ensuring the 
completion of Phase 2 due to which the contractor could not mobilize machinery to the 
site. As a result, only 5 per cent work of Phase-3 could be completed after incurring an 
expenditure of ` 12.62 lakh during 2022-23. Further, commencement of Phase-3 work 
without forest clearance of Phase-2 was not guided by prudent planning. 
(II) As per RIDF Manual, “Annexure – IV, Checklist for preparation of DPRs to be 
submitted for loans under RIDF”, clearance from Department of Environment and 
Forests is to be obtained, wherever necessary, by the State/ UT Government to ensure 
smooth progress of the work. 
The project “Construction of link road from Kathar bridge to Ball Saroh” in Jammu 
was sanctioned in 2010-11 under NABARD17. R&B Construction Division-III Jammu 
allotted (December 2012) the road work to a contractor for ` 39.87 lakh. The status of 
the project was not found on record. Audit noticed that the project “Construction of 
Road from Kathar to Bali Saroh Phase -I (Kund Kanyari) Akhnoor” was again 
sanctioned in 2012-13 under NABARD (RIDF XVIII) at a cost of ` 3.96 crore for km 
1 to 3. The contractor constructed the road up to 1.175 km and thereafter abandoned 
the work after incurring expenditure of ` 2.15 crore (March 2019) due to involvement 
of forest land. 
The project “Up -gradation and widening of link road Kathar Bridge to Ball Saroh  
(length 3 km)” was once again sanctioned (2022-23) under NABARD (RIDF XXVIII) 
at a cost of ` 6.85 crore without ascertaining the status of forest clearance. Though the 
forest clearance, which had earlier led to abandonment of the project, was not available, 
the division floated (June 2023) the tenders for the said work at a cost of ` 6.66 crore. 
The forest clearance  was finally received in November 202 3 resulting in delay in 
execution of the project. 
Hence, in both the cases, executing departments did not comply with the directions of 
NABARD. 
In reply, Department stated (December 2025) that work beyond 1.175 KM could not be 
proceeded solely because of pending statutory clearance from Forest Department. It 
was added that after seeking the forest clearance and technical updation, fresh tenders 
were floated in June 2023 at a cost of ` 6.85 crore and work is in progress.  
The reply does not explain the reasons for not obtaining pre-execution approvals before 
the start of work.
