6.2.4.1  Utilisation of Funds 
The NABARD funds released by the PW (R&B) Department, including loan and State/ 
UT share, were not fully utilised, with expenditure ranging between 33 and 65 per cent 
during 2018-19 to 2022-23. Further, the UT share was not released during 2020-21. In 
21 out of 24 sampled divisions, utilisation of NABARD funds during certain years was 
below 50 per cent, ranging from one to 49 per cent (Appendix 6.1). 
After being pointed out in Audit, the Chief Engineer (CE), PW (R&B) Department 
Jammu stated (July 2023) that full utilization of funds was not practically possible as 
the execution of projects on ground entails various not ideal and unforeseen situations 
such as delayed forest clearance, land disputes, court cases, poor response to NIT 
(notice i nviting tenders), compensation issues, utility shifting by other departments, 
hilly terrain, availability of raw material, weather conditions etc. 
In addition to above reasons for less expenditure, the Department in its reply added 
(December 2025) that the percentage utilisation of funds during 2018 -19 to 2022 -23 
was influenced by several external factors like re -organisation of State and Covid -19 
pandemic. It was added that with normalization of field conditions, expenditure has 
consistently improved from 2022-23 onwards. 
Providing encumbrance free site is imperative before commencement of work, 
otherwise, the Department would continue to get embroiled in the vicious cycle of 
stoppage of work and time and cost overrun. 
6.2.4.2  Irregular expenditure on contingencies 
Para 7.2.2 of Handbook on RIDF envisages that contingency charges under a project 
are permitted up to a maximum limit of 3 per cent  of provision for civil works. 
Government also instructed (January 2021) that contingency charges claimed should 
be incidental and related to works only, and should not be used for hiring of casual 

Chapter-VI: PA on Execution of Works under NABARD-RIDF by Public Works and Jal Shakti Departments 
49 
 
labour (skilled/unskilled) and for payment of wages to need based or work charged staff 
etc. Audit noticed (July to October 2023) in 11 Divisions 18 (out of 24 test -checked 
divisions) that ` 1.68 crore was spent on wages, furniture items, biometric equipment 
etc. in contravention to the Government instructions.  
In reply, Department stated (December 2025) that it has taken cognizance of the audit 
observation and has already sought detailed factual reports from all the concerned  
Chief Engineers to verify such instances and ensure that the responsibilities, if any, are 
appropriately fixed. Besides, instructions have been issued (September 2025) to all field 
offices to prevent recurrence of such deviations. 
6.2.4.3  Diversion of Funds 
As per terms and conditions of Project sanction orders, the funds released are not 
available for re -appropriation/ diversion at any level and should be utilised after 
fulfilling all codal procedures.  
Audit noticed (October 2023) that in 15 road projects (out of 309 test-checked projects) 
in six divisions of five districts 19, ` 3.09 crore was diverted (2012 -13 to 2022 -23) to 
other projects in contravention to the terms and conditions of the sanction orders. 
Similarly, against allotted cost of ` 139.98 crore, an expenditure of ` 1.33 crore was 
incurred (October 2014 to March 2023) on components (culverts, approach wall to 
culvert etc.) outside the scope of DPRs of 14 projects20. 
In reply, Department stated (December 2025) that it has taken firm cognizance of these 
instances. It was also stated that such occurrences were legacy issues of earlier years 
when system-based financial controls were not fully operational. With the launch of the 
Budget Estimation and Management & Allocation System (BEAMS)  integrated with 
JKPaySys, Treasury Net, and JKPWDOMS , diversion of funds has now become 
technically impossible , as the system restricts expenditure strictly to the sanctioned 
project head. 
6.2.5  Contract Management 
Para 6 of Annexure III A: General Terms and Conditions of the Handbook on Rural 
Infrastructure Development Fund (RIDF) outlines specific pre -qualification 
requirements for contractors. Some of the requirements are satisfactory track record of 
the contractor, experience of similar projects executed earlier; contractor not blacklisted 
earlier, adequate banking facilities/ credit limits availed. Further, Para 7.4.2 of 
Handbook on RIDF provides that for road projects, the defect liability period of 
minimum five years is mandatory. 
 
18 Construction Division I Jammu, Construction Division III Jammu and R&B Divisions Poonch, Doda, Nowshera, 
Sopore, Kupwara, Tangdar, Vailoo, Khanabal, Qazigund. 
19 Kashmir: Projects: 13, Division: 4, Districts: 3, ` 1.40 crore; Jammu: Projects: 2, Divisions: 2, Districts: 2,  
` 1.69 crore. 
20 Shopian (4), Budgam (7), Chadoora (2) - 13 projects, ` 1.22 crore; Udhampur – 1 project- ` 0.11 crore. 

Composite Audit Report (Civil and PSEs) for the period ended March 2023  
50 
 
6.2.5.1  Allotment of excess maintenance cost  
As per Para 3.2.7 of the RIDF Manual, maintenance cost for road projects is admissible 
at 10 per cent of construction cost.  Further, the notice inviting tender (NIT) for the 
works also provided that the maintenance cost of the project should be fixed at  
10 per cent of the construction cost.  
Audit noticed (July-September 2023) that in 49 out of 309 test-checked projects, while 
allotting works during June 2021 to June 2023, maintenance cost was added at rates 
ranging from 11 to 18 per cent, exceeding the prescribed limit.  This resulted in excess 
allotment of maintenance cost amounting to ` 3.98 crore. 
In reply, the Superintending Engineers, Rajouri and Doda Circles stated  
(September 2023) that due to competitive bidding, rates quoted were below the 
advertised cost and maintenance cost was added as per NIT conditions. The reply is not 
satisfactory, as addition of maintenance cost in excess of the prescribed 10 per cent 
limit was irregular. 
6.2.5.2  Allotment of contracts without additional performance security 
As per the Jammu and Kashmir Engineering Manual -2020 (J&KEM), in cases of 
unbalanced bids 21, the successful bidder (L1) is required to deposit additional 
performance security, ranging from 5 to 20 per cent of the advertised cost, depending 
on the extent to which the bid is below the advertised cost22.  
In December 2020, GoJK issued circular on performance security, reducing the 
percentage of performance security for all existing contracts to 3 per cent (from existing 
5-10 per cent) and directing that no provisions for Additional Security Deposit to be 
kept in the Bid Documents in case of ‘Abnormally Low bids’. The validity of the 
circular was later (February 2022) extended till 31.03.2023.  
Audit noticed (June–September 2023) that in nine out of 309 test-checked projects, bids 
were 20 to 30 per cent below the advertised cost. Though the validity of the circular 
was till March 2023, these unbalanced bids were accepted and contracts were awarded  
(April 2023 to September 2023 ) without obtaining additional performance security 
amounting to ` 6.52 crore, resulting in undue benefit to contractors. 
On being pointed out, the Superintending Engineer, Baramulla stated (February 2024) 
that a Government of J&K circular (December 2020), applicable up to March 2023, 
dispensed with the requirement of additional performance guarantee for abnormally 
low bids. Further, Department stated (December 2025) that no such corresponding 
provision existed in the GFR 2017 and concerned Superintending Engineer did not seek 
Annual Performance Security at the time of allotment. It was added that most of these 
works stand completed satisfactorily, and no risk or loss has arisen to the Government 
 
21 When bid of the successful contractor is more than 15 per cent below of the advertised cost, then it is called 
unbalanced bid. 
22 Additional performance security: Nil (≤15% below); 5% (>15% –20% below); 10% (>20% –25% below);  
15% (>25%–30% below); 20% (>30% below the advertised cost). 

Chapter-VI: PA on Execution of Works under NABARD-RIDF by Public Works and Jal Shakti Departments 
51 
 
on account of the omission. It was also stated that audit observation has been duly noted, 
and instructions have been explicitly reiterated to all Chief Engineers and field 
formations to ensure strict adherence to the APS requirement under J&KEM in all 
future tendering processes. 
The reply is not satisfactory as the tenders for these works were floated after 
March 2023 i.e., after the expiry of GoJ&K circular and the department should have 
obtained additional performance security from the contractors. 
6.2.5.3  Lesser Defect Liability period 
As per Para 7.4.2 of the RIDF Handbook, a Defect Liability Period (DLP) of five years 
is mandatory for projects sanctioned after 01 April 2020. Audit noticed (August 2023 
to March 2024) that in 39 projects 23 out of 309 test -checked projects sanctioned after 
this date, the DLP specified in NITs floated between October 2020 and August 2023 
ranged from 18 months to three years, instead of the prescribed five years. Inability of 
the PW (R&B) Department to incorporate the mandated DLP in contracts had the 
potential risk of non-maintenance of road projects for the required five-year period after 
completion. 
In reply Department stated (December 2025) that the said instructions regarding the 
Defect Liability Period (DLP) are being strictly adhered to across all zones after  
August 2023. Before August 2023, DLP of works under NABARD was kept less than 
five years on analogy of other roads works. It was added that the Department is in the 
process of issuing Standard Operating Procedures (SOPs)  for the maintenance and 
upkeep of NABARD roads that fall within the DLP as well as beyond it.  
6.2.5.4  Allotment of contracts to ineligible bidder 
As per Para 6(c) of Annexure III-A of the RIDF Handbook, contractors are required to 
have experience of executing similar works. Further, the J&K Engineering Manual 
(J&KEM) provides for two-bid system, wherein only technically qualified bidders are 
eligible for opening of financial bids. 
Two projects, “Construction of road from Eid -gah Marhote to Solian, Poonch” and 
“Construction of road from Kaloon to Haji, Shark Harayala via Dharana, Poonch”, were 
sanctioned under NABARD (RIDF-XXVI) during 2020-21 at a cost of ` 2.92 crore and  
` 2.61 crore, respectively. Audit scrutiny (August 2023) revealed that contracts for both 
projects were allotted (March 2022) to a single contractor who had not furnished 
mandatory evidence of similar work experience and whose technical bids had been 
declared unresponsive, rendering him ineligible for participation in financial bids. 
Despite this, the works were allotted to the ineligible bidder at a cost of ` 1.44 crore 
 
23 Jammu: 20 projects; Kashmir: 19 projects. 

Composite Audit Report (Civil and PSEs) for the period ended March 2023  
52 
 
and ` 1.26 crore against advertised costs of ` 2.84 crore and ` 2.61 crore, respectively. 
An expenditure of ` 0.62 crore24 had been incurred on these projects as of March 2023. 
In reply, the Department stated that the initial allotment in favour of the erstwhile 
contractor was cancelled due to non -performance and the Bank Guarantee amounting 
to ` 3.76 lakh was forfeited as per codal provisions. It was further stated that the balance 
work was re-tendered and allotted (October 2025) to a new contractor and the work is 
in progress. 
The Departmental reply, however, was silent regarding allotment of work to an 
ineligible contractor. 
6.2.5.5  Excess payment to contractors 
Audit noticed that excess payment of ` 10 lakh had been made to contractors in respect 
of two projects in two divisions as detailed in the following paragraphs. 
In respect of road project ‘Dessean to S.K. Bair’ in Udhampur District, Executive 
Engineer, R&B Division Udhampur had made (March 2021 to February 2023) an 
excess payment of ` 10 lakh to the contractor by adding 4.88 per cent above Sanctioned 
Schedule of Rates (SSR) to work done claims. Executive Engineer, R&B Division, 
Udhampur stated (December 2023) that details were being scrutinized in this regard 
and outcome thereof would be intimated to audit. Further, Department stated 
(December 2025) that the work was awarded to the lowest responsive bidder  for  
` 2.14 crore25 and the release of ` 2.13 crore is within the awarded amount and within 
the approved project cost. As such, no excess payment has been made.  
The reply is not acceptable as the contractor submitted bills amounting to ` 2.05 crore, 
on which the Department added 4.88 per cent without any contractual or statutory basis. 
Addition of 4.88 per cent  over the billed amount resulted in excess payment of  
` 10 lakh (4.88 per cent of ` 2.05 crore) to contractor.

earthwork, GSB-filling, WBM-G-II and protection works were again allotted (between 
February 2020 and March 2022) to contractors at a cost of ` 52.50 lakh  under 
NABARD. An expenditure of ` 38.08 lakh was shown incurred on these works during

through a revised DPR and sanction. Accordingly, expenditure of ` five lakh booked 
on two unexecuted culverts remains irregular. 
6.2.6  Execution of Projects 
Para 3 of Handbook on RIDF envisages that the implementation phase for sanctioned 
projects is generally spread over two to five years. A phasing period of four years is 
permitted for projects in J&K. Therefore, Projects sanctioned up to 2018 -19 should 
have been completed by March 2023.  
Audit however, noticed that out of 399 projects sanctioned for J&K, PW (R&B) 
Department under NABARD (RIDF-XX to RIDF-XXIV) at a cost of ` 1,368.27 crore 
during 2014-15 to 2018-19, only 270 projects (68 per cent) had been completed, while  
122 projects were still ongoing as of March 2023. An expenditure of ` 1,097.91 crore 
was shown incurred on these 392 projects. The remaining 7 projects were non -starter 
as of March 2023. 
Further, out of 348 sampled projects, 115 projects were sanctioned under NABARD 
(RIDF-XVIII to RIDF-XXIV) during 2012-13 to 2018-19 at a cost of ` 422.77 crore. 
Out of these 115 projects, four projects were transferred to other schemes. Considering 
the phasing period of four years, these 111 projects should have been completed by  
March 2023. Audit noticed that 73 projects were completed while 38 projects  
(34 per cent) could not be completed within the stipulated period of 4 years. These 
projects had experienced delay ranging between one year and six years. 
In reply, Department stated (December 2025) that out of 399 projects sanctioned under 
NABARD (RIDF -XX to RIDF -XXIV) during 2014 -15 to 2018 -19 at a cost of  
` 1,368.27 crore, 273 projects (68 per cent) have been completed as of December 2025.  
The reply, however, did not provide specific reasons for delayed execution of projects. 
6.2.6.1  Lapse of Loan under closed tranches 
NABARD had sanctioned 145 projects 27 under RIDF XX (2014 -15) and XXI  
(2015-16) in PW (R&B) Department sector for J&K and reimbursement for these 
tranches was allowed up to June 2022 and December 2022 respectively. Audit noticed 
 
27 RIDF-XX (2014-15): 59 projects; RIDF- XXI (2015-16): 86 projects.

(March 2024) that 28 projects28 were still incomplete (March 2023) under these closed 
tranches. As a result, GoJ&K was deprived of balance NABARD loan of ` 34.60 crore 
for these incomplete projects. Further, the public were deprived of the benefits of rural 
connectivity due to these incomplete projects. 
In reply, Department stated (December 2025) that out of 145 projects sanctioned during 
2014-15 and 2015 -16 under NABARD (RIDF -XX and RIDF -XXI), 114 projects  
(79 per cent) have been completed. The funds which were received under NABARD 
loan projects have been utilised as per fund projection for particular financial year. No 
funds under closed trenches got lapsed which could have resulted in denial of benefits 
of rural connectivity to the people.  
The reply, however, does not address the issue of inability to obtain the ` 34.60 crore 
due to projects remaining incomplete as of March 2023.  
Moreover, non-completion of these projects also led to deferment of intended benefits 
of rural connectivity to the public.

6.2.6.3  Excess Execution of items of works 
As per Para 3.2.23 of the RIDF Manual, only minor deviations that do not affect the 
scope or benefits of a project may be permitted by NABARD. Further, the conditions 
of allotment stipulate that quantities of individual items shall not exceed the advertised 
quantities, the total cost shall not exceed the allotted cost, and no change in proposals 
or specifications is permissible without prior written approval of the competent 
authority. 
Audit noticed that in 30 projects (out of 309 test -checked projects) sanctioned under 
NABARD (RIDF-XX to RIDF-XXVII), quantities of works amounting to ` 2.96 crore 
were executed (2014-15 to 2021-22) in excess of the allotted works in the range from  
11 per cent to 1,028 per cent and 4,414 per cent in one case without prior approval of 
NABARD (Appendix 6.2).  
In reply Department stated (December 2025) that due to unforeseen site conditions and 
urgent operational requirements , minor adjustments were made during execution to 
ensure structural safety without altering project scope or exceeding the sanctioned cost. 
It was also stated that the Department acknowledges that such deviations should have 
been routed through the regional office of NABARD as prescribed under Manual on 
RIDF and as stipulated in the conditions of the allotment orders. Accordingly, 
instructions have already been reiterated to all Executive Engineers to seek prior 
approval for any variation in quantities or design changes. Further, a compliance report, 
detailing corrective measures and vetting of deviations, shall be submitted to NABARD 
for confirmation. The Department will ensure strict adherence to the stipulated 
procedures in future. 
 
 

Chapter-VI: PA on Execution of Works under NABARD-RIDF by Public Works and Jal Shakti Departments 
57 
 
6.2.6.4  Project completion certificates and Project Completion Reports 
According to Para 23(b) of Handbook on RIDF, the implementing department has to 
submit a Project Completion Certificate (PCC) to the NABARD within one month on 
completion of the project and based on receipt of PCC, NABARD is to treat the project 
as complete in its records. Further, as per Para 13.2, the Project Completion Report 
(PCR)29 is a document of information, which is to be submitted by the implementing 
department in the prescribed format to the regional office of NABARD within 
six months from the date of completion of a project. Further, a State/ UT has to assess 
the potential created for generation of income and employment in the areas where the 
projects have been executed and the same is to be reflected in PCRs along with expected 
and indirect benefits.  
Audit noticed that out of 81 completed projects 30(out of 348 test -checked projects), 
PCC of only one project had been submitted within the prescribed time of one month 
while PCCs of 16 completed projects had been submitted by six Implementing 
Divisions31 with delay ranging from two months to four years. PCCs were not submitted 
for the remaining 64 completed projects. 
PCRs of 11 completed projects out of 81 completed projects were submitted without 
complete details as envisaged in NABARD guidelines and PCRs of the 70 projects were 
not submitted at all. Therefore, the potential created by NABARD projects for 
generation of income and employment in the areas where projects were executed could 
not be estimated. 
In reply, Department stated (December 2025) that it has taken cognizance of audit 
observation and now it has become a regular feature to submit PCC/ PCR to NABARD 
within one month. 
6.2.6.5  Projects transferred to other Schemes 
Six projects (out of 309 test-checked projects) with a sanctioned cost of ` 21.82 crore, 
on which expenditure of ` 6.81 crore was incurred under NABARD during 2012-13 to  
2022-23, were subsequently transferred by the PW (R&B) Department to the Pradhan 
Mantri Gram Sadak Yojana (PMGSY). Transfer of projects after availing NABARD 
loans diluted scheme -specific funding objectives, resulted in interest liability on the 
loans drawn, and indicated weak internal controls. 
Further, the project “Improvement/ Upgradation of Tutian Di Khui to Khada Madana 
road Phase-II (km 9 to 11)” was sanctioned under NABARD during 2019 -20 at a cost 
of ` 2.97 crore (Loan: ` 2.67 crore; UT share: ` 29.72 lakh), despite having already

been proposed (September 2017) under the World Bank -aided Jehlum Tawi Flood

The above indicates significant lapses in internal controls, as the same project was 
sanctioned under different schemes, resulting in avoidable interest liability on 
NABARD loan funds. 
In reply, Department stated (December 2025) that p roject sanctioned under other 
schemes has been de -sanctioned by the NABARD and loan availed in respect of the 
projects transferred to other schemes has not been utilized and NABARD has adjusted 
the loan amount of the de-sanctioned projects against other ongoing projects. 
The reply is not satisfactory as transfer of projects to other schemes after sanction and 
partial drawal of NABARD loans diluted the scheme -specific funding objective and 
resulted in avoidable interest liability, irrespective of subsequent adjustment of lo an 
amounts by NABARD. Further, sanction of the same project under NABARD and 
JTFRP reflects weak internal controls in project planning and scheme selection. 
6.2.6.6  Projects with forest, land and other issues 
Para 22 of Handbook on RIDF stipulates that (a) the State/ UT Government shall take 
all necessary steps to remove any legal or other procedural hurdles in the smooth 
implementation of the projects and (b) State/ UT Government shall ensure completion 
of all processes including land acquisition and obtaining necessary clearances from the 
concerned authorities to enable timely completion of sanctioned projects.  
Audit noticed that 36 sampled projects  (initiated during January 2013 to March 2013 ) 
out of 309 test -checked projects in 16 divisions of 8 districts with project cost of  
` 136.72 crore (Loan: ` 122.86 crore and UT share:  ` 13.86 crore) had not fulfilled 
formalities like forest clearance, land acquisition and others. Consequently, these 
projects remained incomplete (March 2023). Against release of loan of ` 63.74 crore 
for these projects, an expenditure of ` 37.53 crore was incurred during 2012 -13 to  
2022-23. 
In reply, Department stated (December 2025) that t he delays primarily arose from 
external regulatory constraints , especially prolonged forest clearances, local land 
disputes, and court interventions, which are common in ecologically sensitive regions 
of UT of J&K and were not attributable to departmental inefficiencies. While ensuring 
adherence to environmental safeguards, it was stated that the Department pursued 
resolution through regular follow -up with the Forest Department via the PARIVESH 
portal and engagement with local authorities, and that most impediments have since 
been resolved, enabling completion of several projects now open to traffic. 

Chapter-VI: PA on Execution of Works under NABARD-RIDF by Public Works and Jal Shakti Departments 
59 
 
The fact remained that RIDF clearly requires the State/ UT Government to complete 
land acquisition and obtain all statutory clearances to ensure timely completion of 
projects. The fact that 36 projects remained incomplete due to pending forest clearance, 
land acquisition, and related issues indicates inadequate pre -sanction planning and 
weak project preparedness. 
6.2.7  Quality Control 
According to Para 23 of Handbook on RIDF, the Project Implementing Authority is to 
undertake desk/ field monitoring and quality control tests 32 on material and finished 
works. The reports of the inspecting officer, quality control tests and compliance thereto 
are to be retained on records by the concerned work Executing division. Further, 
Para 1(4) of Annexure III B: Other Conditions of Handbook on RIDF provides that 
State/UT Government has to ensure availability of technical personnel and well 
equipped laboratory system for quality control. Periodic tests on material and finished 
works are to b e conducted as per IRC/Ministry of Road Transport and Highways. 
Moreover, the UT Government has to ensure availability of adequate number of mobile 
testing vans with a laboratory. 
Audit noticed (July 2023 to January 2024) that the results of quality control tests had 
not been maintained for 288 sampled projects 33. Mandatory tests required to be 
conducted had not been prescribed in the tender documents and test reports of 
mandatory tests were not enclosed with the final bills of 81 completed projects. Only 
in respect of one project in PW Construction Division -I Jammu, three types of test 
reports34 were attached with the works bills.  
In reply, the Department while acknowledging the audit observation stated  
(December 2025) that non-availability of quality control test records was due to legacy 
deficiencies in record -keeping, limited laboratory infrastructure in remote divisions, 
and absence of a unified quality monitoring framework during the earlier period. It was 
added that field engineers were conducting routine inspections and material checks, 
however documentation of test results were not standardized across divisions. It was 
stated that a Two -Tier Quality Control Mechanism, including third -party inspections, 
has been instituted (February 2022), with DIQC designated as the nodal agency, and 
that field laboratories are being strengthened to ensure proper testing and 
documentation in future. 
6.2.8  Monitoring and Inspections 
Para 23 of Handbook on RIDF provides that monitoring of progress of works is also to 
be done by NABARD through prescribed returns, review meetings, high power

committee meetings and periodic field visits. Further, Para 4.4 of Manual on RIDF 
states that the UT/ State Governments have their own mechanism for monitoring which 
includes monitoring by their Engineers/ Officers and maintenance of visit registers, 
test reports etc.  
Audit observed that periodicity of field visits was neither specified in NABARD 
guidelines nor prescribed by the Departmental authorities. In respect of 191 sampled 
projects35 jointly inspected by Audit with Departmental officers, the inspection notes 
and site order books/ visit register detailing inspections carried out by Departmental 
authorities (Executive Engineers, Superintending Engineers, Chief Engineers) had not 
been pr epared indicating that inspections carried out, if any, were not documented 
properly. Further, no monitoring of projects was carried out by the NABARD officials 
in any of the sampled divisions.  
In reply, the Department stated (December 2025) that departmental engineers 
conducted regular site inspections, but in some cases inspection notes and visit registers 
were not formally recorded, resulting in documentation gaps rather than lack of field 
monitoring. It was further stated that a work -wise Quality Control Register is being 
introduced at the Division level and instructions have been issued to ensure proper 
maintenance of inspection records in compliance with RIDF guidelines. 
6.2.8.1  Third party Monitoring 
Para 1(4)(h) of Annexure III B of Handbook on RIDF provides that to ensure quality 
control third party monitoring (TPM) of projects is to be insisted during execution stage 
at regular intervals. Government of Jammu and Kashmir  appointed (January 2020) 
Directorate of Inspection and Quality Control (DIQC) for TPM which was to conduct 
full physical verification of roads and bridges projects with sanctioned cost of  
` five crore and above and on test-check basis of projects with sanctioned cost less than 
` five crore.  
Audit noticed that TPM was not carried out by DIQC or any other authority in the 
sampled R&B Divisions. 
Department stated (December 2025) that although DIQC was designated as the nodal 
agency in 2020, third -party monitoring across PW (R&B) was operationalised from 
February 2022, and inspections of NABARD projects are now being carried out 
accordingly. It was  added that the strengthened framework ensures compliance with  
Para 1(4)(h) of Annexure III -B of the Handbook on RIDF and safeguards quality 
control requirements. 
6.2.8.2  Public Disclosure Requirements not complied 
Para 14 of the Handbook on RIDF stipulates that to bring the basic details of projects 
in public domain and generate awareness among ultimate beneficiaries, media,

Department stated (December 2025) that observation has been noted and instructions

6.2.9  Non production of records

pertaining to NABARD as provided (September 2025) by Superintendent Engineer 
Rajouri to Chief Engineer Jammu. However, reply was silent with regard to delay of 
more than two years in submission of documents and non -furnishing of documents by 
the Superintending Engineer Jammu Circle.

that on receipt of funds for a project from Finance Department, project Implementing

PW (R&B) Department in January 2024. 
In reply, the Department stated (December 2025) that the observation has been noted 
with seriousness and the concerned Executive Engineers have been directed 
(September 2025) to trace, compile, and furnish all pending vouchers. Besides,

6.3  Jal Shakti Irrigation and Flood Control Department (I&FCD) 
A total of 52 projects39 were being executed in Jal Shakti Irrigation and Flood Control 
Department (I&FCD) during 2018 -23 (under RIDF -tranche XVII to XXVII 40). Of 
these, execution of 20 projects 41 spread across selected 12 Divisions 42 in 10 districts43 
of J&K, was examined in Audit. The findings are discussed in the succeeding 
paragraphs. 
6.3.1  Planning for selection of projects for funding under NABARD 
Paragraph 2.1.4 of Manual on RIDF provides that State/ UT Governments are expected 
to prioritize projects under a tranche and submit suitable proposals of eligible activities/ 
projects with DPRs to NABARD for sanction. 
Audit noticed that none of the 12 selected Divisions of I&FCD maintained baseline 
data or a documented mechanism for selection and prioritisation of projects in line with 
the provisions of RIDF, or for deciding whether projects should be taken up under 
NABARD, Capex Budget or Centrally Sponsored Schemes such as AIBP. Instead, 
NABARD proposals were made on an ad hoc basis, largely driven by requests from 
local farmers and recommendations of public representatives. 
During the Exit Conference (October 2025), the Department stated that project 
selection was influenced by availability of funds from different sources and 
recommendations of public representatives. It further stated that priority projects had 
been proposed for NABARD funding in recent years.

As seen from Table 6.3.1 , against a total release of ` 92.77 crore during 2018 -23, 
expenditure incurred was ` 53.58 crore (58 per cent), leaving ` 39.19 crore unutilised. 
The unutilised portion of released funds during each year ranged between 27 and

records. 
Audit further noted that the Chief Engineer (CE), I&FCD Jammu released ` 4.43 crore 
during 2018 -19 to 2021 -22 for execution of five out of eleven selected projects of 
Jammu Division to five executing Divisions 44 after time-lags45 ranging from two to  
46 days, whereas no such delay occurred during 2022 -23. Similarly, the CE, I&FCD 
Kashmir released ` 77.49 crore during 2018-19 to 2022-23 to executing Divisions with 
time-lags of up to 60 days. These delays in release of funds affected execution of 
projects to that extent. 
In reply, the Jal Shakti Department stated (July 2025/ October 2025) that 
underutilisation of funds during 2018 -19 to 2022 -23 was due to extraordinary 
circumstances, namely abrogation of Article 370, the COVID -19 pandemic, and 
manpower shortages. The Depar tment further stated that delays in fund release were 
unintentional and attributable to administrative procedures and inter -departmental 
coordination. 
6.3.2.1  Non-Utilisation of Mobilisation Advance 
Six NABARD-assisted projects 46 under I&FCD were sanctioned (December 2020 to  
March 2022) at a cost of ` 35.09 crore, with loan assistance of ` 31.60 crore. NABARD 
released Mobilisation Advance of ` 9.48 crore to the Finance Department of GoJ&K 
for these six projects during the said period.  
Audit noted that the Finance Department released only ` 3.82 crore (40 per cent) in 
September 2021 and January 2023 to I&FCD whereas ` 5.66 crore was not released. 
Out of ` 3.82 crore47, the concerned Divisions utilised only ` 0.05 crore (one per cent) 
as of March 2023 . Thus, ` 9.43 crore of the NABARD loan released could not be 
utilised.

6.3.2.2 Short Release of State/ UT share 
Against the required State/ UT Share of ` 7.92 crore for 35 projects sanctioned during 
2018-20, only ` 1.42 crore (18 per cent ) was released by GoJ&K, resulting in 
short-release of ` 6.50 crore as of August 2023. This short -release of State/ UT share 
adversely impacted the timely execution of these projects. Further, no State/ UT share 
amount was released by GoJ&K during 2020-23.  
6.3.3  Project execution 
Audit findings relating to execution of 20 selected projects of I&FCD are discussed in 
the succeeding paragraphs. 
6.3.3.1  Deficiencies in preparation of DPRs 
As per the RIDF Manual of NABARD, the State/ UT Government has to ensure 
completion of all processes including land acquisition, obtaining necessary clearances 
from the concerned authorities etc. to enable timely completion of the sanctioned 
projects. The projects should be technically feasible, financially viable and have an 
Economic Rate of Return (ERR)48 greater than 10 per cent and the Benefit- Cost Ratio 
(BCR)49 has to be more than one at a discounting factor of 10 per cent. 
Audit noted that in nine (45 per cent ) out of 20 selected projects, No Objection 
Certificates (NOCs) of Forest Department and State Ground Water Authority and 
rainfall hydrology data were not obtained  before sanction of their DPRs. Further, in 
three projects50, Benefit-Cost Ratio had been incorrectly calculated51. 
In reply, the Jal Shakti Department stated (July 2025) that the NOC from the Forest 
Department had been obtained and those from other departments were under process. 
It also attributed the BCR discrepancy to human error during data compilation and 
stated that the recalculated BCR of 2.89 is above the prescribed threshold of 1.5. 
During the Exit Conference (October 2025), the Department assured that the 
deficiencies pointed out would be examined and necessary corrective measures would 
be undertaken. 
 
48 Economic Rate of Return (ERR) provides a single metric showing a project's economic benefits compared to its 
costs. 
49 Benefit- Cost ratio analysis is a process of evaluating a project by systematically comparing its expected benefits 
against its costs. It helps determine whether the potential gains outweigh the financial and other costs. 
50 Said Ladi Canal of ID Shopian, Mirpora Dugpora Canal and Sumbal Canvas Canal of I&FC Division Ganderbal. 
51 Benefit-Cost Ratio (CBR) was calculated on gross production at the post -construction stage, whereas it should 
have been calculated on additional production. 

Chapter-VI: PA on Execution of Works under NABARD-RIDF by Public Works and Jal Shakti Departments 
65 
 
6.3.3.2  Non-compliance of rules in seeking approval of the Projects from 
J&KWRRA 
Paragraph 51 of Chapter V of J&K State Water Resources Regulatory Authority 
(Regulations for Water Usage Charges and Approval of Schemes) prescribes that any 
user intending to install a unit requiring usage of water for the purpose of generation of 
electricity; irrigation of land; drinking purposes (domestic, commercial, industrial or 
institutional); running of water mills; and exploration and exploitation of ground water 
shall prepare a scheme and submit to the J&K Water Resources Regulatory Authority 
(J&KWRRA) accompanied by such fee and service charges as may be fixed by the 
authority for approval. 
Audit noted that the approval of J&KWRRA was not obtained by the concerned 
Executive Engineer, I&FCD  Division for any of the 80 I&FC projects (Jammu: 62; 
Kashmir: 18) submitted for sanction under NABARD during 2018 -23. This indicates 
required importance was not accorded to the prescribed rules. 
J&KWRRA also confirmed (January 2024)  to Audit that it is responsible to approve 
and review water resources schemes/ projects and it is binding upon all utilities to 
submit projects to the authority for approval. It added that the matter was brought to the 
notice of the Principal Secretary to GoJ&K, JSD in May 2022 but response was 
awaited. 
During the Exit Conference, the Department assured that a circular would be issued 
making it mandatory to obtain the approval of J&KWRRA for all relevant projects. 
6.3.3.3  Indiscriminate splitting of works 
As per Para 1.10 (iii) of Manual of Procurement of Works 2019, Department of 
Expenditure, GOI, no project or work will be split up to bring it within the sanctioning 
powers of a lower authority. 
Audit noticed that during the period from August 2011 to May 2023, in 13 out of the  
20 selected projects 52 executed across 12 Divisions, the Department split them into 
multiple smaller works and allotted among 276 contractors at a total cost of  
` 21.14 crore instead of awarding them as a single contract. The amounts of individual 
contracts ranged between ` 0.07 lakh and ` 94.57 lakh. This splitting of works resulted 
in fragmented execution and monitoring. The details of splitting of works are given in 
Appendix 6.3. 
Jal Shakti Department stated (July 2025) that fragmentation of tenders was done with 
the intent to ensure timely utilisation of funds and simultaneous commencement of 
works at multiple spots to ensure timely completion of projects. The decision to split 
the works was not intentional avoidance of composite tendering but rather aligned with 
the Department’s established practices at that time. 
The reply does not explain the reason for non-compliance of the prescribed rules. 
 
52 Jammu: 09; Kashmir: 04. 

Composite Audit Report (Civil and PSEs) for the period ended March 2023  
66 
 
6.3.3.4 Execution of works for the project “Sandran and Vethvethroo” 
The project “Flood protection works on tributaries of Sandran and Vethvethroo Nallah” 
was approved (January 2017) under NABARD funding at a cost of ` 5.64 crore and 
executed by the Executive Engineer, FCD, Anantnag. Following site visits, the 
Executive Engineer reported (August 2020) that changes in the course of tributaries 
necessitated modification of the DPR, and the Chief Engineer simultaneously dir ected 
that tenders should not be invited without approval of the Administrative Department. 
However, a committee headed by the  same Chief Engineer allowed (October 2020) 
adoption of the modified proposals without NABARD approval, and a revised DPR for 
` 5.23 crore was submitted in January 2021. 
Subsequently, tenders were invited (February 2021), work was allotted (March 2021) 
for ` 3.25 crore, and Technical Sanction was accorded by the CE belatedly (April 2021). 
An expenditure of ` 3.14 crore (Loan: ` 3.06 crore; UT share: ` 7.76 lakh) was incurred 
as of October 2023. 
In reply, the Department stated (July 2025) that modifications were necessary and that 
Technical Sanction had been accorded. 
However, approval of the revised proposal from the Administrative Department/ 
NABARD had not been obtained as of July 2025, rendering the expenditure irregular. 
Joint Physical Verification revealed that works were executed at scattered locations 
across different tributaries and did not constitute a composite flood protection project. 
No signage boards indicating NABARD funding were installed at the sites, and in some 
locations, signage of the Rural Development Department was found, obscuring project 
ownership. The works identified at site were mainly erosion control works, such as 
cement concrete, crate and stone works, and were not of flood protection or critical 
nature. 
In reply, the Department stated (July 2025) that erosion control works were executed at 
vulnerable locations susceptible to flash floods and that signage boards would be 
installed in future. 
Audit further noticed that no evidence of change in the course of Sandran and 
Vethvethroo Nallah was found at the sites, as claimed by the Executive Engineer. Site/ 
work-wise estimates and drawings were not prepared, and Measurement Books were 
not maintained for individual works. The Executive Engineer stated (April 2024) that 
estimates were prepared for aggregated quantities and therefore Measurement Books 
were not maintained for works executed at different locations.  
6.3.3.5  Execution of project “Sumbal Canvas Canal” along slide prone areas 
A project “Construction/ Modernisation of Sumbal Canvas Canal” was sanctioned  
(July 2011) at a cost of ` 2.24 crore by NABARD for irrigation of surrounding villages. 
The I&FC Division Ganderbal received (2011 -12 to 2017-18) loan and State share of  

Chapter-VI: PA on Execution of Works under NABARD-RIDF by Public Works and Jal Shakti Departments 
67 
 
` 2.24 crore53 which was spent. Out of this, during 2015-16 to 2017-18, an expenditure 
of ` 1.31 crore54 was booked in the name of Sumbal Canvas Canal, on construction of 
the conduits, conductor/ cunette cutting, construction of supply channel, removal of 
malba, making good damaged site, etc. 
Audit examination revealed that neither Administrative Approval nor Technical 
Sanction had been accorded for the project, and the work was allotted in a piecemeal 
manner at Division level to 58 contractors. Further, a fresh DPR for “Construction/  
Modernisation of Sumbal Canvas Canal” costing ` 60.38 lakh was submitted 
(December 2018) to the Chief Engineer/ Government for sanction under NABARD; 
however, approval was awaited as of November 2023. 
Joint Physical Verification (August 2023) revealed that 2 -3 km of the canal lay in  
slide-prone areas, remained earthen with crate work, was blocked by heavy debris, and 
had been defunct since 2014, casting doubt on the authenticity of reported works. 
Additionally, construction of a PMGSY road altered the canal alignment over about 
1,800 metres, and in the absence of a flume 55 over the Nallah, the canal could not 
function as intended. 
Thus, improper selection of gravity project along slide prone area had rendered 
expenditure of ` 2.24 crore idle, besides depriving farmers of intended benefits of the 
project for over one decade.  
In reply, the Department stated (July 2025) that sanction of projects without formal 
Administrative Approval was a prevailing practice up to 2019 and that relevant records 
were not available as the Directorate office was inundated during the 2014 floods. The 
project had achieved 41.54 per cent financial progress up to March 2015, continued up 
to 2017-18, and that efforts were underway to revive the canal. It stated that a DPR for 
restoration was framed in December 2018, which is still awaiting approval.

specifically allotted for the NABARD assisted projects. 
(II) No Objection Certificate of Forest Department was not obtained prior to 
sanction (2021 -22) of the project “LIS Gantmulla” in I&FC Division Baramulla

6.4  Public Health Engineering Department 
A total of 344 NABARD assisted rural water supply projects were implemented in the  
Jal Shakti (Public Health Engineering/PHE) Department (JSD) during 2018 -2023. 
These projects included 263 projects 56 sanctioned under NABARD (RIDF -tranche 
XXII to XXVI) during 2016-17 to 2020-2157. Out of total 344 projects, 102 projects 58 
with sanctioned cost of ` 242.49 crore across 23 Divisions59 in 10 districts of J&K were 
selected for audit. The audit findings are discussed in the succeeding paragraphs. 
6.4.1  Planning for selection of projects  
As per NABARD Guidelines (September 2021), the State/UT Government is required 
to prepare a Plan of Action (State Plan) for ensuring supply of safe drinking water to 
the designed population, including uncovered rural areas. Projects are to be prioritised 
on the basis of this State Plan and supported by DPRs before being submitted to 
NABARD through the Nodal Department for loan sanction. 
Audit noted that no State/ Union Territory plan  for providing safe drinking water to 
uncovered rural areas had been prepared by the JSD (PHE) as of March 2023.  
In reply, the Department stated (August 2025) that the DPRs of the Schemes were 
prepared on the basis of actual requirements and were prioritised in order of the level 
of necessity. It was added that the recommendations made by the Hon’ble CM, Hon’ble 
Ministers, Hon’ble MLAs and elected representatives of PRIs were helpful in 
identifying the priority areas.  
During the Exit Conference (October 2025), the Department stated that the project 
selection was influenced by availability of funds from various funding sources and 
added that in recent years, priority projects had been proposed for NABARD funding.

6.4.3.1  Utilisation of funds 
It can be seen from Table 6.4.2 that there was poor utilisation of funds during 2018-19 
to 2022-23, as against release of ` 844.17 crore, JSD (PHE) incurred an expenditure of 
only ` 382.50 crore (45 per cent) resulting in savings of ` 461.67 crore (55 per cent). 
The year-wise savings during 2018-23 ranged between 4 and 72 per cent. 
Due to poor utilisation of funds, there was no substantial progress in completion of 
projects, as only 32 out of 263 projects were completed which were due for completion 
by March 2023. Audit noted that the delay in release of funds to the implementing 
divisions contributed to poor utilisation of funds. 
Further, against the total amount of ` 805.17 crore 64 released by Chief Engineers 
(Kashmir and Jammu), 34 implementing divisions reported savings of ` 422.70 crore65 
during 2018-23, indicating utilisation of only 47 per cent of the released funds. The 
savings of such significant funds were indicative of poor financial management and 
inadequate monitoring by the Department. As a result, the projects could not be 
completed within stipulated time -frame, besides putting undue int erest burden on the 
State exchequer.

divisions.

In reply, Department stated (August 2025) that poor response to composite tendering

` 39 crore by the Chief Engineer, without any recorded reasons/documentation was not 
addressed.  
6.4.3.2  Utilisation of loan 
As per NABARD guidelines, a project is treated as a non -starter if work is not 
commenced within 12 months of sanction, irrespective of release of Mobilisation 
Advance (MA). A project is considered grounded only after issue of work order and 
commencement of  physical work; in absence of which, the sanction lapses after  
18 months and any MA released is required to be adjusted or recovered. 
Audit noticed that NABARD had sanctioned (2018 -23) 19 water supply projects 67  
(out of the total 263 sanctioned) at a cost of ` 81 crore68, to be completed by March 2023 
and aimed to provide safe drinking water to a population of 1.93 lakh.  However, these 
19 projects were not started despite NABARD releasing  loan of ` 32.01 crore. 
Consequently, GoJ&K could not receive further funding of ` 49 crore from NABARD. 
In reply, Department stated (August 2025) that due to composite tendering the projects 
could not be started. It was added that in October 2023, condition of the composite 
tendering was relaxed and these works are now at final stage of completion. 
Not ensuring timely initiation of the projects within the stipulated period of 18 months 
despite availability of funds deprived the beneficiaries of the intended benefits.

(1.34 per cent) was incurred on items of works (maintenance, repairs and other works72) 
which did not form part of approved DPRs for these projects. Further, ` 1.24 crore73

6.4.3.4  Blockade of funds  
The Executive Engineer, PHE Division Doda credited (March 2018) ` 20.46 lakh 
pertaining to the NABARD project “Water Supply Scheme (WSS) Bari” to the Deposit 
Head (Major Head -8443 Civil Deposits). The amount remained parked under the 
deposit head, resulting in non -completion of the project as of March 2023 against the 
scheduled completion of March 2020. The records did not indicate the reasons for 
crediting the funds to the deposit head; however, this may have been done to avoid 
surrender of funds. 
On being pointed out in Audit, Department stated (August 2025) that amount was 
deposited in civil deposit head in March 2018, but due to dispute among the local 
people, the work was delayed. The dispute was resolved in February 2022 and  
90 per cent of work was completed. 
The reply was, however, silent on utilisation of funds deposited in civil deposit head. 
6.4.4  Project implementation 
NABARD guidelines stipulate that projects should be selected after comprehensive 
study of technical, economic, financial, and organisational aspects to ensure that capital 
resources are used to create productive assets, which are expected to realise benefi ts 
over a period of time. Risk factors in project implementation such as land acquisition, 
forest clearance and other bottlenecks are to be identified at the time of project 
proposals and suitable steps taken for mitigation of risks for timely completion o f 
projects. The normal phasing for implementation of projects under RIDF is four years 
for J&K. Instances of not observing these requirements in 23 test-checked divisions of 
JSD (PHE) and their impact are discussed in the succeeding paragraphs. 
6.4.4.1  Allotment of project without approved design  
The JSD accorded (February 2018) administrative approval to the project  
“WSS Bagh-i-Nowgam”, Anantnag at a cost of ` 2.95 crore, targeted for completion 
by March 2021, to provide safe drinking water to six habitations with a population of 
2,710. 

Chapter-VI: PA on Execution of Works under NABARD-RIDF by Public Works and Jal Shakti Departments 
73 
 
Audit examination of records of the Executive Engineer, JSD Bijbehara revealed that 
the key component of the project, construction of the Overhead Tank (OHT), was 
allotted (June 2019) to a contractor at a cost of ` 0.65 crore without an approved design, 
due to which the work could not be started. The design was subsequently approved in 
February 2020, increasing the cost of the OHT to ` 0.91 crore, and the contractor 
commenced work in August 2020. However, the Department decided (March 2023) 
that the contractor would execute the work only at the originally allotted cost of  
` 0.65 crore, and the remaining works would be re -tendered. As of March 2023, 
expenditure of ` 2.55 crore had been incurred on the project, which was only  
76 per cent  physically completed. The project could not be commissioned as of  
August 2025. 
While accepting the audit observation, the Department stated (August 2025) that the 
work was allotted without approved design as the same was awaited from the Design, 
Inspection and Quality Control Department (DIQC), and that the design was approved 
later with cost escalation due to bearing capacity considerations.  It was added that the 
remaining portion of work was taken up under JJM and delayed due to pending land 
compensation.  
Thus, inability of the Department to obtain approved design before allotment of work 
resulted in significant delay, leaving the project incomplete even after two years beyond 
the stipulated completion date, led to cost escalation of ` 0.26 crore, and deprived  
six habitations with a population of 2,710 of safe drinking water  during the period of 
delay. 
6.4.4.2  Defective Detailed Project Reports  
According to NABARD’s RIDF guidelines, the DPRs are to be submitted for all 
projects and the projects must be technically feasible and economically viable. Audit 
noted the following deficiencies in this regard: 
(I) The project “WSS Nagbani” was sanctioned (August 2018) under NABARD 
(RIDF-XXIV) at a cost of ` 3.92 crore, with stipulated completion by March 2021. 
Audit examination disclosed that although the DPR proposed drilling of a tube well 
near the existing one, no provision was made for acquisition of land . Nevertheless, 
funds were advanced (November 2018 to January 2019) by the Chief Engineer, Jammu 
to the Mechanical Division for procurement of material and to the Ground Water 
Drilling Division for drilling of the tube well. An expenditure of ` 1.28 crore was 
booked in 2018 -19 and 2019 -20 as advances to sister divisions and Small -Scale 
Industries Development Corporation Ltd. (SICOP) for procurement of material. 
However, no progress was made in construction of the tube well due to absence of land 
acquisition provision in the DPR. 
In reply, the Department stated (August 2025) that the scheme was foreclosed and its 
scope covered under the Jal Jeevan Mission (JJM). It further stated (October 2025) that  
` nine lakh paid to SICOP for procurement of Tar Steel would be utilised in 

Composite Audit Report (Civil and PSEs) for the period ended March 2023  
74 
 
forthcoming tenders, and that ` 57 lakh advanced to the Executive Engineer, Jal Shakti 
(PHE) Mechanical Ground Water & Drilling Division, Jammu could not be utilised for 
drilling at Nagbani due to site -related issues and was instead utilised for drilling tube 
wells under other ongoing NABARD schemes. 
The fact remains that the funds were not utilised on the intended scheme. 
(II) The project “WSS Batala” was sanctioned (September 2020) under NABARD 
(RIDF-XXVI) at a cost of ` 10.59 crore, and administrative approval was accorded 
(November 2020) at ` 10.57 crore. The DPR proposed a bore well with an anticipated 
discharge of 35,000 gallons per hour. However, the selected site (CPS Boria) already 
had six tube wells supplying other WSSs, none of which had a discharge of 
35,000 gallons per hour, and drilling of an additional tube well would have adversely 
affected the existing sources. 
Further, the proposal to lay a 200 mm rising main of 6,800 m length up to a head of  
275 m for direct conveyance of water to the existing Ground Service Reservoir 
(capacity: one lakh gallons) without any intermediate stage was found to be practically 
unviable. 
Consequently, a revised DPR for ` 10.28 crore was prepared proposing two tube wells 
of 10,000 gallons per hour at two locations (Gladni and near Ravidass Temple, Boria) 
with staged lifting. Although technical sanction was accorded (November 2022), 
execution had not commenced as of March 20 23, resulting in non -utilisation of  
` 3.61 crore released during 2020-21 and 2021-22 due to preparation of a faulty DPR. 
While accepting the audit observation, the Department stated (August 2025) that 
execution was delayed due to non-availability of a viable water source as envisaged in 
the DPR, and that drilling of one tube well under the revised DPR was under progress. 
During the Exit Conference (October 2025), the Department assured corrective action. 
6.4.4.3  Execution of projects without ensuring encumbrance free land 
Audit noted that in respect of three sampled WSSs, execution of projects had been 
started without ensuring encumbrance free land. As a result, safe drinking water could 
not be provided to a population of 9,596 despite expenditure of ` 7.24 crore as detailed 
in Table 6.4.3.

6.4.4.4  Execution of project without identification of feasible site 
Audit noted that projects were executed without identification of feasible sites as 
discussed in the following paragraphs: 
(I) The project “WSS Rakyeen” was sanctioned (February 2017) under NABARD 
(RIDF-XXII) at a cost of ` 3.19 crore, with a target date of completion of March 2020, 
to provide safe drinking water to four villages (Balkote, Silikote, Mothal and Thandi 
Kasse) having a population of 3,159. Audit examination revealed that construction of a 
key component, the filt ration plant, was allotted (January 2018) but could not be 
commenced due to landslide in the area. During 2016 -17 to 2022-23, JSD (PHE) Uri 
incurred an expenditure of ` 2.45 crore on other components such as service reservoir 
and pipe networks; however, the project was only 77 per cent complete. Despite lapse 
of six years since allotment of work, alternate land for construction of the filtration 
plant had not been identified as of February 2024, rendering the scheme non-functional. 
In reply, the Department stated (August 2025) that the project had been physically and 
financially closed and that the infrastructure created under NABARD was integrated 
with a retrofitting scheme under JJM (“WSS Soura Hathlanga”) to provide potable 
water. 
However, in the monthly progress report of March 2026, the Department has shown the 
work of “WSS Rakyeen” as ‘still in progress’ which is contrary to reply furnished in 
August 2025.  
(II) The project “WSS Narwal” was sanctioned (February 2020) under NABARD 
(RIDF-XXV) at a cost of ` 4.74 crore, with completion targeted for March 2023. Audit 
noticed that funds of ` 1.03 crore (2021-22) and ` 1.50 crore (2022-23) were released 
to PHE-II, Jammu and the Ground Water Drilling Division (GWDD), Jammu for civil 
works and drilling of tube well, respectively. However, the GWDD could not drill the 
tube well as the selected site ( Panchayat Ghar ) was not approachable for heavy 
machinery due to inadequate road width. Consequently, no work for development of 
the water source could be executed and ` 2.53 crore remained unutilised as of  
August 2023. 
In reply, the Department stated (August 2025) that a fresh survey was conducted, a new 
site was identified, drilling was completed and remaining works were under progress. 
The fact, however, remained that due to wrong selection of site for development of the 
water source, the project remained incomplete as of August 2025. 
6.4.4.5  Deficient execution of projects 
Audit noticed that deficiencies in execution of projects resulted in not providing of safe 
drinking water to targeted population, as discussed in the following paragraphs: 
(I) The project “WSS Neejbal Markundal” was sanctioned (March 2018) under 
NABARD (RIDF-XXIII) at a cost of ` 3.98 crore for providing safe drinking water to  

Chapter-VI: PA on Execution of Works under NABARD-RIDF by Public Works and Jal Shakti Departments 
77 
 
six habitations of Neejbal, Markundal areas having population of 4,500. The stipulated 
date of completion of project was March 2021. 
Audit examination disclosed that the division incurred an expenditure of ` 1.32 crore74 
on the project during 2017-23. The execution of the project was stopped as the cost of 
the work had escalated due to preparation of estimates based on outdated Schedule of 
Rates (SoR) of 2012. One of the key components of the scheme i.e., clear water sump 
cum pump house, without which the scheme cannot be commissioned , was not 
incorporated in the original DPR . This component was subsequenlty proposed  
(June 2023) for execution under Jal Jeevan Mission (JJM) scheme . Thus, the project 
could not be commissioned despite incurring an expenditure of ` 1.32 crore. 
In reply, Department accepted (August 2025) the audit finding and stated that remaining 
component had been taken up under retrofitting in JJM. It was also stated that the work 
was expected to be completed soon.   
(II) The project “WSS New Colony Mamath”, Budgam was sanctioned 
(August 2018) under NABARD (RIDF -XXIV) at a cost of ` 1.12 crore to provide 
drinking water to two areas with a population of 1,350, with stipulated completion by 
March 2021. Audit noted that, in violation of NABARD guidelines mandating 
e-tendering, the pipe distribution system was allotted (June –October 2019) to 
five contractors through manual tendering. Pipe material of 2,800 metres of 100 mm 
GI pipes valuing ` 21 lakh was issued (2019-20) to the contractors. However, in view 
of Government instructions (December 2019) requiring adoption of e -tendering, 
payments could not be made to the manually selected contractors. 
Although the Department issued repeated notices, latest in August 2023, for return of 
the pipe material, the contractors demanded payment for handling and shifting of pipes, 
and no action was taken till February 2024 to cancel the contracts and re -tender the 
works. Consequently, no further work was executed and expenditure of ` 0.87 crore 
incurred during 2018 -23 was rendered unfruitful, depriving a population of 1,350 of 
safe drinking water. 
In reply, the Department accepted (August 2025) that the pipe material had been issued 
but not laid, resulting in non-completion of the scheme. It further stated (October 2025) 
that the matter for recovery of pipe material had been taken up with the Police  
Department, with a copy endorsed to the Deputy Commissioner, Budgam, and that the 
process of blacklisting the contractors had been initiated. The Department also stated 
that part of the population was covered under Jal Jeevan Mission and the remaining 
coverage was proposed under District CAPEX. 
(III) The project “Improvement and augmentation of WSS Tandwal, Rajouri” was 
sanctioned (August 2018) under NABARD (RIDF -XXIV) at a cost of ` 3.73 crore, 
with stipulated completion by March 2021, to provide adequate water supply to a 
 
74 ` 54.96 lakh on procurement of pipes, Chain link fencing and laying of 1,000 m rising main of 150 mm and  
` 76.60 lakh on purchase of mechanical equipment and machinery. 

Composite Audit Report (Civil and PSEs) for the period ended March 2023  
78 
 
population of 2,202 in five areas (Tandwal, Patan Mohra, Karian, Mubarakpura and 
Chawa), including augmentation for areas facing depletion of discharge from the 
existing dug well. 
Audit examination revealed that although the DPR proposed Sukh Tawi River as the 
water source, the scheme was modified to drilling of two bore wells due to  
non-availability of land for a filtration plant. As of March 2023, expenditure of  
` 2.78 crore had been incurred on drilling of two bore wells, installation of pumping 
machinery, construction of pump rooms and electric substations. While bore well  
No. 2 was made functional, bore well No. 1 remained non-functional as of August 2023 
due to non -installation of mechanical components and non -laying of the rising main, 
resulting in inability to achieve the intended objective even after lapse of over two years 
beyond the stipulated completion date. 
In reply, the Department stated (August 2025) that the electric substation had been 
installed at bore well No. 1 and installation of mechanical components was under 
progress, with overall physical progress at 70 per cent. The reply, however, needs to be 
viewed in light of the fact that despite lapse of more than four years beyond the 
stipulated completion date, the project was only 70 per cent complete. 
(IV) The project “WSS Labertal Chandpore”, Budgam was sanctioned  
(August 2018) under NABARD (RIDF-XXIV) at a cost of ` 1.74 crore to provide water 
to four habitations (Labertal, Latipora, Garindcheck and Chandpore) with a population 
of 5,486, using Sukhnag Nallah as the water source, with stipulated completion by 
March 2021. Audit examination revealed that JSD (PHE) Divisi on Budgam incurred 
an expenditure of ` 1.64 crore and reported the project as 92 per cent complete as of 
December 2023. However, against the DPR requirement of 24,850 metres of pipeline, 
only 6,072 metres had been issued to contractors, leaving 18,778 metres (76 per cent) 
unissued, indicating either incorrect reporting of physical progress or a defective DPR. 
In reply, the Department stated (August 2025) that the scheme was completed and 
commissioned except one habitation (Latipora), where distribution lines could not be 
laid due to conflict among villagers. However, fact remains that the scheme was 
reported as completed without laying the envisaged 76 per cent of pipeline. 
6.4.5  Incorrect reporting on completion of project 
The project “WSS Hanjura” was sanctioned (February 2017) under NABARD  
(RIDF-XXII) at a cost of ` 3.27 crore and scheduled to be completed by 
March 2020.The Executive Engineer, JSD (PHE) Division Chadoora incurred a n 
expenditure of ` 2.91 crore during 2018 -23 on the project. It was noted that land 
compensation of ` 0.50 lakh was disbursed to the landowner against the provision of 
` 25 lakh in the DPR. Due to inability to pay land compensation, landowner did not 
allow further execution of works. As such, project could not be made functional. 
However, the project was wrongly shown as completed in the records  of the Division 
as on October 2023. 

Chapter-VI: PA on Execution of Works under NABARD-RIDF by Public Works and Jal Shakti Departments 
79 
 
In reply, Department accepted (August 2025) that the scheme was still incomplete and 
erroneously shown as completed. 
6.4.6  Execution of projects without Technical Sanction 
As per NABARD Guidelines, Technical Sanction (TS) is required to be accorded within 
three months of project sanction to ensure that estimates are based on proper surveys 
and that designs are technically feasible. Audit noticed that out of 263 projects 
implemented during 2018–23, TS had not been accorded in 232 projects (88 per cent), 
indicating that execution of these works lacked assurance of accurate estimates and 
proper surveys. 
In reply, the Department stated (August 2025) that, except for a few turnkey projects, 
almost all NABARD projects were executed after accord of TS. The reply is not 
satisfactory, as TS had not been granted in the majority of projects up to March 2023 
and no documentary evidence was furnished to substantiate the contention. 
6.4.7  Monitoring and inspections 
NABARD guidelines provide for monitoring of projects by both NABARD and the 
departmental authorities through periodic field visits.  
Audit noted that inspection notes or site order books in support of the inspections 
carried out by departmental authorities (Executive Engineers, Superintending 
Engineers and Chief Engineers) were not prepared/ maintained by work executing 
divisions. This indicates that inspections, if any, carried out by higher authorities were 
not documented. Besides, NABARD had also not carried out any inspection in respect 
of 75 ongoing sampled projects during the period 2018-19 to 2022-23. 
In reply, Department stated (August 2025) that  the robust monitoring and inspection 
system exists in the Department and proper documentation process was in place. 
However, no supporting documents were furnished with reply. 
6.5  Conclusion 
Against GoI consent for raising loans of ` 3,900 crore from NABARD, GoJ&K availed 
only ` 2,316.37 crore (59 per cent ) during 2018 -19 to 2022 -23. Disbursement by 
NABARD ranged between 28 and 84 per cent across tranches, primarily due to slow 
progress of works. In several cases, loans lapsed due to non -adherence to prescribed 
timelines. 
Delays in release of Mobilisation Advance (MA), retention of MA by the Finance 
Department, excess release in some projects, and non -utilisation of MA were noticed. 
These deficiencies adversely affected cash flow and timely execution of projects. 
Project planning and prioritisation were weak across the executing departments. 
Baseline data and State/ UT -level planning frameworks envisaged under NABARD 
guidelines were either absent or not effectively used for project selection. Mandatory 

Composite Audit Report (Civil and PSEs) for the period ended March 2023  
80 
 
pre-requisites such as availability of encumbrance -free land, forest and statutory 
clearances, identification of viable water sources, and approval of technically sound 
DPRs were not ensured before sanction or execution, leading to stalled, abandoned or 
foreclosed projects. 
Execution of projects suffered from systemic deficiencies, including delays in release 
of funds, non-commencement and slow progress of works, diversion and misutilisation 
of funds, execution without Technical Sanction, defective DPRs, improper site 
selection and inadequate contract management. Monitoring and inspection mechanisms 
were weak, as periodic field inspections were either not conducted or not documented. 
As a result, a significant proportion of projects remained incomplete, with substantial 
time and cost overruns, and the intended objectives of irrigation and flood management, 
provision of safe drinking water and improvement of rural road connectivity  were not 
achieved within the envisaged timeframes. Overall, there is need for strengthening 
institutional planning, financial discipline, inter -departmental coordination, technical 
scrutiny and monitoring mechanisms to ensure effective utilisation of NABA RD 
assistance and timely delivery of intended benefits. 
The citizen -centric objective of NABARD funding to provide low -cost funding to 
State/UT Governments for completing delayed rural infrastructure projects, bridging 
the rural urban gap and promoting inclusive growth, covering areas like irrigation, 
roads, health, education and water, is yet to be realized. 
6.6  Recommendations 
The Government may consider that the: 
• Timelines for release of Mobilisation Advance to Implementing Departments 
by Finance Department may be prescribed in sanction order in consultation 
with NABARD. 
• System may be developed to prevent occurrence of release of Mobilisation 
Advance twice for same project. 
• Pre-execution approvals from various authorities before initiating the 
projects are ensured.

Finance Department 
7.1  Integrated Financial Management System in Jammu and Kashmir 
7.1.1  Introduction  
The Integrated Financial Management System (IFMS) was envisaged under Mission 
Mode Project (MMP) of the National e-Governance Plan (NeGP). It is a comprehensive 
financial management system for ensuring effective financial management and  
real-time monitoring of the fiscal position  of the State. To attain these objectives , the 
Government of Jammu and Kashmir (GoJ&K) took up (March 2010) implementation 
of IFMS.  
GoJ&K selected National Informatics Centre (NIC), New Delhi to formulate a proposal 
for implementation of Integrated Financial Management System (IFMS) in Jammu and 
Kashmir. The selection was made after reviewing the scope of work done by NIC in 
Maharashtra and Karnataka and its prior experience in implementing IFMS in other 
States.  
Accordingly, NIC submitted (April 2010) the draft project proposal which inter-alia 
included electronic integration with external stakeholders like Reserve Bank of India 
(RBI), Banks, Accountant General ’s office, Life Insurance Corporation, Income Tax 
Department and others.  
After incorporating the requirements of the erstwhile State in the project proposal,  
Finance Department (FD), GoJ&K prepared (January 2011) a Detailed Project Report 
(DPR) for implementation of project. The DPR was vetted by the  
Information & Technology Department of GoJ&K and was submitted (January 2011) 
to Ministry of Finance, Government of India by FD, GoJ&K for approval.  
The Ministry of Finance, Government of India (GoI) approved the project and released 
the first instalment of funds for implementation of project in March 2011.  In terms of 
cabinet decision dated 19 October 2011, the Implementation of IFMS in 121 treasuries1 
was planned in three2 phases. 
 
 
 
1  Implemented in 121 treasuries as per Revised GoJ&K Treasury Code notified vide no. S.O. 53 dated  
17 February 2021 of Finance Department, GoJ&K.  
2  Phase-I: District level and Muffasil treasuries; Phase -II: Sub -treasuries and Phase -III: Remote  
Sub-treasuries. 

Composite Audit Report (Civil and PSEs) for the period ended March 2023  
 
82 
 
The objectives of IFMS project are as follows:  
• Monitoring and controlling of State revenue and expenditure.  
• Effective management of the State’s finances so that funds are optimally utili sed 
for various citizen centric projects.  
• Effective and improved delivery of services related to pension processing, fund 
accounting and maintenance of H ouse Building Advance/ Motor Car Advance 
(MCA) accounts.  
• Managing the Budget process and timely release of grants/ funds to Government 
departments/ organisations.  
• Effective support and coordination with other departments. 
• Interfacing with Jammu and Kashmir Bank Limited (J&KBL)/ Reserve Bank of 
India and Accountant General’s office regarding payments/ receipts, finali sation 
of State accounts and managing grants/ funds received from Government of India 
(GoI) and other agencies.  
• Facilitating the Drawing and Disbursing Officers (DDOs) to track their budget 
allotment and payments. 
• Putting in place an effective Management Information System (MIS) for decision 
making. 
• Maintaining financial accounting and facilitating automated account generation 
across the State/ Union Territory (UT).  
Twelve modules initially incorporated in Detailed Project Report are listed in  
Table 7.1.1 . While the Mission Mode Project guidelines proposed inclusion of  
four additional modules out of w hich C&AG interface module and E–Audit were not 
implemented.
